242.5947pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 242.5947pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(T_{0}(Q),\mathcal{O}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 347.69766pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 347.69766pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

By purity of
F
and Proposition
2.6
, we deduce
T_{0}(Q)=0
, which implies that
Q
is pure 1-dimensional.
∎

For
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs with Gorenstein support, we also have the following characterization.

Proposition 2.11
.

For any
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pair
(F,s)
on
X
with Gorenstein support
S
and non-zero cokernel
Q
, we have a natural quasi-isomorphism

(7)

F^{\vee}\otimes K_{X}[n-2]\cong[\mathcal{O}_{S}\to Q^{D}\otimes K_{S}^{\vee}]\otimes K_{S}

where
Q^{D}:=\curly Ext^{n-1}_{X}(Q,K_{X})
is a pure 1-dimensional sheaf supported on
S
.
Moreover, this induces an equivalence between
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs supported on
S
with non-zero cokernel and 1-dimensional
\mathrm{PT}
pairs on
S
.

Proof.

Since
S
is Gorenstein, we have
R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{S},K_{X})\cong K_{S}[-(n-2)]
.
Moreover,
Q
is pure 1-dimensional by Proposition
2.10
.
Applying the functor
R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{X}(-,K_{X})
to the short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{S}\to F\to Q\to 0
, we obtain a distinguished triangle

\textstyle{F^{\vee}\otimes K_{X}[n-2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{\vee}\otimes K_{X}[n-1],}

which implies the desired quasi-isomorphism (
7
).

Since
Q
is pure 1-dimensional, we have
Q\cong Q^{DD}
and
Q^{D}
is pure 1-dimensional as well. Moreover
F
is pure, hence the cokernel
\curly Ext_{X}^{n-1}(F,K_{X})
of the map
K_{S}\to Q^{D}
is 0-dimensional. It is easy to see that all these operations are invertible thereby establishing an equivalence.
∎

The equivalence in Proposition
2.11
implies that
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs on Gorenstein (and smooth) supports have simple descriptions. Fix a 2-dimensional pure subscheme
S
of
X
.

Corollary 2.12
.

Assume that
S
is Gorenstein. A
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pair
(F,s)
on
X
with support
S
and non-zero cokernel is equivalent to a pair
(C,t)
where
C\subset S
is a pure 1-dimensional subscheme and
t:\omega_{C}\twoheadrightarrow R
is a 0-dimensional quotient.

Proof.

By the previous lemma, we may view a
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pair on
X
with support
S
and non-zero cokernel as a 1-dimensional
\mathrm{PT}
pair on
S
. The support of this stable pair is a 1-dimensional pure subscheme
C\subset S
and the stable pair is determined by a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to G\to Q\to 0,

where
G
is pure 1-dimensional and
Q
is 0-dimensional. Applying
R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{S}(-,K_{S})
and using
\curly Ext^{2}_{S}(G,K_{S})=0
(purity of
G
), we obtain a quotient

t:\omega_{C}=\curly Ext_{S}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{C},K_{S})\twoheadrightarrow\curly Ext^{2}_{S}(Q,K_{S}).

Note that applying
R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{S}(-,K_{S})
again, and using the fact that
\mathcal{O}_{C}^{DD}\cong\mathcal{O}_{C}
and
\mathcal{O}_{C}^{D}
is pure 1-dimensional, we can recover the original 1-dimensional
\mathrm{PT}
pair on
S
, and hence
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pair on
X
. ∎

Corollary 2.13
.

Assume that
S
is smooth. A
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pair
(F,s)
on
X
with support
S
and non-zero cokernel is equivalent to a pair
(C,Z)
of an (non-zero) effective divisor
C\subseteq S
and a 0-dimensional subscheme
Z\subseteq C
.

Proof.

Since any pure 1-dimensional subscheme
C
of a smooth surface
S
is a local complete intersection,
C
is Gorenstein and
K_{C}
is invertible. Hence the proof follows directly from Corollary
2.12
.
∎

2.4.
Numerical bounds

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
n
with fixed very ample line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)
. For any coherent sheaf
F
on
X
, we denote its Hilbert polynomial by

P_{F}(t)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\alpha_{i}(F)\cdot\frac{t^{i}}{i!},

where
\alpha_{0}(F)=\chi(F)
is the Euler characteristic. In this subsection, we derive boundedness results on
\alpha_{i}(F)
for
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs
(F,s)
which are crucial in the GIT construction in subsection
3.2
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Fix rational numbers
\alpha_{2}>0
and
\alpha_{1}
.

(1)

For each
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
, the set

\{\alpha_{1}(F):\text{$\mathrm{PT}_{q}$ pairs $(F,s)$ such that $\alpha_{2}(F)=\alpha_{2}$}\}

is bounded below.

(2)

The two sets

\{\chi(F):\text{$\mathrm{DT}$ pairs $(F,s)$ such that $\alpha_{2}(F)=\alpha_{2}$, $\alpha_{1}(F)=\alpha_{1}$}\}

\{\chi(F):\text{$\mathrm{PT}_{0}$ pairs $(F,s)$ such that $\alpha_{2}(F)=\alpha_{2}$, $\alpha_{1}(F)=\alpha_{1}$}\}

are bounded below, and the set

\{\chi(F):\text{$\mathrm{PT}_{1}$ pairs $(F,s)$ such that $\alpha_{2}(F)=\alpha_{2}$, $\alpha_{1}(F)=\alpha_{1}$}\}

is bounded above.

Proof.

(1) The
\mathrm{DT}
case is shown in
[
Pot1
, Cor. 2.13]
. The
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
case follows directly from the
\mathrm{DT}
case by considering the short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to F\to Q\to 0
since
\alpha_{2}(F)=\alpha_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
and
\alpha_{1}(F)=\alpha_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
. Finally, the
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
case also follows directly from the
\mathrm{DT}
case by considering the short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{S}\to F\to Q\to 0
since
\alpha_{2}(F)=\alpha_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{S})
and
\alpha_{1}(F)\geq\alpha_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{S})
.

(2) (
\mathrm{DT}
) Choose a
\mathrm{DT}
pair
(\mathcal{O}_{Z},1:\mathcal{O}_{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Z})
such that
\alpha_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\alpha_{1}
, and
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})<0
. Consider the map

(8)

(X\setminus Z)^{[-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})]}\to\operatorname{Hilb}(X,\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t):W\mapsto Z\sqcup W

from the Hilbert scheme of points to the Hilbert scheme of closed subschemes with Hilbert polynomial
v=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t
. Since the map (
8
) is injective and the Hilbert scheme
\operatorname{Hilb}(X,v)
is of finite type, the Euler characteristic
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
has a lower bound which only depends on
X
,
\alpha_{2}
, and
\alpha_{1}
.

(
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
) Given a
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pair
(F,s)
, we can form a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
Z
is the support of
F
and
Q
is the cokernel of
s
. Since

\alpha_{2}(F)=\alpha_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{Z}),\quad\alpha_{1}(F)=\alpha_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z}),\chi(F)=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})+\chi(Q),

a lower bound in the case (
\mathrm{DT}
) also gives us a lower bound for the case (
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
).

(
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
). Choose a
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pair
(F,s)
and form a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
S
is the support of
F
and
Q
is the cokernel of
s
. By Lemma
2.15
(2) below, it suffices to show that
\mu_{\min}(F)
is bounded below. Note that there exists a surjective map
F\twoheadrightarrow F_{\min}
to a 2-dimensional
\mu
-semistable sheaf
F_{\min}
such that
\mu_{\min}(F)=\mu(F_{\min})
. We can assume that
F_{\min}
is pure. We can form an induced short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{\min}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

for a subscheme
Z\subseteq S
and a quotient sheaf
Q\twoheadrightarrow R
. Note that we have

\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{O}_{S})\leq\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\leq\mu(F_{\min})=\mu_{\min}(F)

(cf.
[
HL
, Lem. 1.3.3]
). Since
S
is pure, the minimal slope
\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{O}_{S})
is bounded below by
[
Pot1
, Cor. 2.13]
. Therefore
\mu_{\min}(F)
is also bounded below.
∎

We need the following lemma to complete the proof of Proposition
2.14
(2) for
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety with a fixed very ample line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)
and let
\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}
be rational numbers such that
\alpha_{2}>0
.

(1)

The set of integers

\left\{\alpha_{0}:\begin{matrix}\text{$\mu$-stable reflexive sheaves $F$ on $X$ such that}\\
\text{$P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}$}\end{matrix}\right\}

is bounded above.

(2)

For a fixed rational number
C
, the set of integers

\left\{\alpha_{0}:\begin{matrix}\text{pure sheaves $F$ on $X$ such that}\\
\text{$P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}$ and $\mu_{\min}(F)\geq C$}\end{matrix}\right\}

is bounded above.

Proof.

(1) The proof is similar to
[
GK
, Prop. 3.6(2)]
. Choose a
\mu
-stable reflexive sheaf
F
with
P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}
. Assume that
\alpha_{0}>0
. Let
Z
be the support of
F
. Let
U\subseteq Z^{\mathrm{red}}
be a non-empty smooth connected open subscheme of the reduced subscheme
Z^{\mathrm{red}}
of
Z
. After shrinking
U
to a possibly smaller non-empty open subscheme, we may assume that
F|_{U}\cong\mathcal{O}_{U}^{\oplus r}
is a trivial vector bundle on
U
.

We first claim that there is a map

\Phi:U^{[\alpha_{0}]}\to M^{\mu}(X,\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t)

from the Hilbert scheme of points on
U
to the moduli space of
\mu
-stable pure sheaves on
X
such that there is a short exact sequence

(9)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

for all
W\in U^{[\alpha_{0}]}
. Indeed, let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq U^{[\alpha_{0}]}\times U\subseteq U^{[\alpha_{0}]}\times X
be the universal family. Then we have a surjective map
\mathcal{O}_{U^{[\alpha_{0}]}}\boxtimes F|_{U}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{U^{[\alpha_{0}]}\times U}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
induced by some projection map
\mathcal{O}_{U}^{\oplus r}\to\mathcal{O}_{U}
. Hence we can form a short exact sequence

(10)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{U^{[\alpha_{0}]}}\boxtimes F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

of sheaves on
U^{[\alpha_{0}]}\times X
, which are flat over
U^{[\alpha_{0}]}
. Hence
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
defines the map
\Phi
and by taking the fibres of (
10
) over
W\in U^{[\alpha_{0}]}
, we obtain (
9
).
From (
9
) and the fact that
F
is reflexive, it follows that
\Phi(W)
is pure 2-dimensional and
\Phi(W)^{D}\cong F^{D}
.
Moreover,
\Phi(W)\cong F\in\mathrm{Coh}_{2,1}(X):=\mathrm{Coh}_{\leq 2}(X)/\mathrm{Coh}_{\leq 0}(X)
so that
\Phi(W)
is
\mu
-stable.

We then claim that the map
\Phi
is injective. Choose two 0-dimensional closed subschemes
W_{1},W_{2}\subseteq U
and let
F_{i}=\Phi(W_{i})
for both
i=1,2
. Assume that there is an isomorphism
\phi:F_{1}\xrightarrow{\cong}F_{2}
of sheaves. Then we can form a commutative square

\textstyle{F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{F_{1}^{DD}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{DD}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{F_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{2}^{DD}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

since
F_{1}^{DD}\cong F^{DD}\cong F_{2}^{DD}
and
F
is reflexive. Since
F
is
\mu
-stable,
F
is stable, and thus simple, i.e.
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(F,F)=\mathbb{C}
. Therefore by replacing
\phi
by
c\cdot\phi
for some constant
c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we may assume that the dotted arrow
\psi
is the identity map
\operatorname{id}_{F}
.
Hence
F_{1}\cong F_{2}
as subsheaves of
F
, and thus the cokernels are isomorphic,
\mathcal{O}_{W_{1}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{W_{2}}
. Therefore
W_{1}=W_{2}
and
\Phi
is injective.

Since
\Phi
is injective and
\dim(U^{[\alpha_{0}]})\geq\dim(U)\cdot\alpha_{0}
, the Euler characteristic
\alpha_{0}
is bounded above by the dimension of the finite type scheme
M^{\mu}(X,\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t)
. This proves (1).

(2)
Case 1.
We first show that

\{\alpha_{0}:\text{ $\mu$-stable pure sheaves $F$ on $X$ such that $P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}$}\}

is bounded above. Let
F
be a
\mu
-stable pure sheaf with
P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}
. Since
F
is pure, we can form a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{DD}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

for some 0-dimensional sheaf
R
by
[
HL
, Prop. 1.1.10]
. Then
F^{DD}
is a reflexive
\mu
-stable sheaf with
P_{F^{DD}}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+(\alpha_{0}+\chi(R))
. Since
\alpha_{0}(F^{DD})=\alpha_{0}+\chi(R)
is bounded above by Lemma
2.15
(1),
\alpha_{0}(F)
is also bounded above.

Case 2.
We then show that

\{\alpha_{0}:\text{ $\mu$-semistable pure sheaves $F$ on $X$ such that $P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}$}\}

is bounded above. Choose a
\mu
-semistable pure sheaf
F
with
P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}
. Consider the Jordan-Hölder filtration

0=F_{0}\subseteq F_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq F_{l}=F

of
F
with respect to
\mu
-stability
[
HL
, Thm. 1.6.7(ii)]
. The length
l
of the filtration is bounded from above because the leading term
\alpha_{2}
of the Hilbert polynomial is fixed. Since
F
is pure, we can assume that all
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
are also pure. Since each
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
is a
\mu
-stable pure sheaf with the slope
\mu(F_{i}/F_{i-1})=\mu(F)
, Case 2 follows from Case 1.

Case 3.
Finally, we consider the general case. Let
F
be a pure sheaf with
P_{F}(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}t^{2}+\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{0}
. Choose a Harder-Narasimhan filtration

0=F_{0}\subseteq F_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq F_{l}=F

of
F
with respect to the
\mu
-stability
[
HL
, Thm. 1.6.7(i)]
. Since
F
is pure, we may assume that
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
are
\mu
-semistable pure sheaves. If
\alpha_{2}(F)
,
\alpha_{1}(F)
are fixed and
\mu_{\min}(F)
is bounded below, then there are only finitely many possible
\alpha_{1}(F_{i}/F_{i-1})
(and
\alpha_{2}(F_{i}/F_{i-1})
). Therefore

\alpha_{0}(F)=\sum_{i}\alpha_{0}(F_{i}/F_{i-1})

is also bounded above by the result in Case 2.
∎

3.
Moduli spaces

In this section, we construct moduli spaces of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs via geometric invariant theory and prove that they are open subschemes of the moduli space of perfect complexes parametrizing polynomially Bridgeland stable objects.

3.1.
Moduli stack of pairs

In this subsection, we introduce the moduli stack of all pairs and prove that the
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
-stability conditions are bounded and open conditions.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety over
\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

We define the
moduli stack of pairs on
X
as a 2-functor

\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X):\mathrm{Sch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{Groupoid}

sending a scheme
T
to the groupoid of (not necessarily 2-dimensional) pairs
(F,s)
of
T
-flat coherent sheaves
F
on
X\times T
and sections
s\in\Gamma(X\times T,F)
. The morphisms
(F_{1},s_{1})\to(F_{2},s_{2})
in the groupoid are isomorphisms
\phi:F_{1}\to F_{2}
of sheaves such that
\phi(s_{1})=s_{2}
.

(2)

We define the
moduli stack of non-zero pairs on
X
as the substack

{\curly Pair}(X)\subseteq\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)

consisting of the pairs
(F,s)
such that
s_{t}\neq 0\in\Gamma(X,F_{t})
for all
t\in T
.

The moduli stacks
\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)
and
{\curly Pair}(X)
are algebraic stacks. Moreover, they have explicit descriptions as unions of open substacks which are global quotient stacks. We will now review the proofs of these facts.

Let us first fix some notation. Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
n
with a fixed very ample line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)
. Fix
v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
and let
P_{v}(t)\in\mathbb{Q}[t]
be the associated Hilbert polynomial. Choose an integer
m
. Fix a vector space
V
of dimension
P_{v}(m)
. Let

(11)

\mathcal{Q}:={\curly Quot}(X,V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m),v)

be the Quot scheme of quotients
\rho:V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m)\twoheadrightarrow F
with
\operatorname{ch}(F)=v
. Let

(12)

V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{F}

be the universal quotient on
\mathcal{Q}\times X
and let
\pi:\mathcal{Q}\times X\to\mathcal{Q}
be the projection map. Consider the open subscheme

(13)

\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}:=\Big\{(F,\rho)\in\mathcal{Q}:V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H^{0}(X,F(m))\text{ and }H^{i}(X,F(m))=0\,\forall i>0\Big\},\

where
V\to H^{0}(X,F(m))
is the map induced by
\rho
. Then the canonical map

(14)

V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to R\pi_{*}(\mathbb{F}(m))

induced by (
12
) is an isomorphism over
\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The moduli stack
{\curly Pair}(X)
has the following properties:

(1)

We have a decomposition

{\curly Pair}(X)=\bigsqcup_{v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})}{\curly Pair}(X,v)

by disjoint open substacks
{\curly Pair}(X,v)
consisting of the pairs
(F,s)
on
X
with
\operatorname{ch}(F)=v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
.

(2)

We have

{\curly Pair}(X,v)=\bigcup_{m\geq 0}{\curly Pair}(X,v)_{m}

for open substacks
{\curly Pair}(X,v)_{m}
consisting of the pairs
(F,s)
on
X
such that
F(m)
is globally generated and
H^{>0}(X,F(m))=0
.

(3)

We have a global quotient stack presentation

{\curly Pair}(X,v)_{m}=\left[\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})/\mathrm{PGL}(V)\right],

where
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F}):=\operatorname{Proj}\,\operatorname{Sym}h^{0}((R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})^{\vee})
denotes the projective cone.

Consequently, the moduli stack
{\curly Pair}(X)
is an algebraic stack (locally of finite type).

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Let
{\curly Coh}(X)
denote the moduli stack of coherent sheaves on
X
. Then

{\curly Coh}(X)=\bigsqcup_{v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})}{\curly Coh}(X,v){\curly Coh}(X,v)=\bigcup_{m\geq 0}{\curly Coh}(X,v)_{m}

for open substacks
{\curly Coh}(X,v)_{m}\subseteq{\curly Coh}(X,v)\subseteq{\curly Coh}(X)

such that

\displaystyle{\curly Coh}(X,v)(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\{F\in{\curly Coh}(X)(\mathbb{C}):\operatorname{ch}(F)=v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})\},

\displaystyle{\curly Coh}(X,v)_{m}(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\left\{F\in{\curly Coh}(X,v)(\mathbb{C}):\begin{matrix}F(m)\text{ globally generated and }\\
H^{i}(X,F(m))=0\text{ for all }i>0\end{matrix}\right\}.

Let
\mathbb{F}^{C}
be the universal family on
{\curly Coh}(X)\times X
and let
\pi^{C}:{\curly Coh}(X)\times X\to{\curly Coh}(X)
be the projection map. Then
R\pi^{C}_{*}(\mathbb{F}^{C}(m))
is a vector bundle over
{\curly Coh}(X,v)_{m}
and the associated
\mathrm{GL}_{P_{v}(m)}
bundle is exactly
\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}
. Hence we have a global quotient stack presentation (see also
[
LMB
, Thm. 4.6.2.1]
)

{\curly Coh}(X,v)_{m}=\left[\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}/\mathrm{GL}(V)\right].

By definition, the moduli stack of pairs is the abelian cone

\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)=C(\curly Ext^{n}_{\pi^{C}}(\mathbb{F}^{C},K_{X}))=\operatorname{Spec}\,\operatorname{Sym}\curly Ext^{n}_{\pi^{C}}(\mathbb{F}^{C},K_{X})

associated to the coherent sheaf
\curly Ext^{n}_{\pi^{C}}(\mathbb{F}^{C},K_{X})=h^{0}((R\pi^{C}_{*}\mathbb{F}^{C})^{\vee})
on
{\curly Coh}(X)
and the moduli stack of non-zero pairs is the complement

{\curly Pair}(X)=\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)\setminus 0_{{\curly Coh}(X)}\subseteq\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)

of the zero section
0_{{\curly Coh}(X)}:{\curly Coh}(X)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)
. Hence, we have (1) and (2) for the open substacks
{\curly Pair}(X,v):={\curly Pair}(X)\times_{{\curly Coh}(X)}{\curly Coh}(X,v)
and
{\curly Pair}(X,v)_{m}:={\curly Pair}(X)\times_{{\curly Coh}(X)}{\curly Coh}(X,v)_{m}
. Moreover, we have a global quotient stack presentation

{\curly Pair}(X,v)_{m}=\left[(C_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(\curly Ext^{n}_{\pi}(\mathbb{F},K_{X}))\setminus 0_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}})/\mathrm{GL}(V)\right]=\left[\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})/\mathrm{PGL}(V)\right]

since
\mathrm{PGL}(V)=\mathrm{GL}(V)/\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists an open substack
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
of
{\curly Pair}(X,v)
, for any
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
, such that the
\mathbb{C}
-points are given by

{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)(\mathbb{C})=\{(F,s)\in{\curly Pair}(X,v):\text{$(F,s)$ is $\mathrm{PT}_{q}$-stable}\}.

Moreover, the moduli stack
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
is an algebraic space of finite type.

We will prove Proposition
3.3
through three steps. We first prove that
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs are
simple
pairs.

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair
(F,s)
on
X
, we have

\operatorname{Aut}_{{\curly Pair}(X)}(F,s)=\{1\}.

Proof.

Consider the short exact sequence of sheaves

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
Z
is the support of
F
and
Q
is the cokernel of
s
. By the
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
-stability condition, we have
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(Q,F)=0.
Hence we have an injective map

(15)

\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(F,F)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{Z},F)=\Gamma(X,F):\phi\mapsto\phi(s).

Since
\operatorname{Aut}_{{\curly Pair}(X)}(F,s)
is the fibre of the above map (
15
) over the point
s\in\Gamma(X,F)
, the automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}_{{\curly Pair}(X)}(F,s)
is trivial.
∎

Secondly, we prove that the collection of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs is
bounded
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The collection of sheaves

\{F\in{\curly Coh}(X,v)(\mathbb{C}):\text{$\exists s\in\Gamma(X,F)$ such that $(F,s)$ is $\mathrm{PT}_{q}$-stable}\}

for fixed
X
and
v
is bounded in the sense of
[
Gro2
, Def. 1.1]
.

Proof.

The boundedness of the
\mathrm{DT}=\mathrm{PT}_{-1}
pairs follows from
[
Gro2
, Thm. 2.1]
and the boundedness of the
\mathrm{PT}=\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs follows from
[
Pot1
, Thm. 4.11]
. Thus it suffices to consider the
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pairs.

Given a
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pair
(F,s)
with
\operatorname{ch}(F)=v
, let
Z
be the support of
F
and
Q
the cokernel of
s
. By Proposition
2.14
(2) for
\mathrm{DT}
-stability,
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
is bounded below. On the other hand
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
is bounded above since
\chi(Q)\geq 0
. Moreover,
\operatorname{ch}_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\operatorname{ch}_{i}(F)
for
i<n
. Hence there are only finitely many possible Chern characters
\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
, and the collection of all such
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
is bounded by
[
Gro2
, Thm. 2.1]
.
Therefore, there exists an integer
m
such that for all
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
as above, we have
H^{i}(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z}(m-i))=0
for all
i>0
. For any
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pair
(F,s)
with
\operatorname{ch}(F)=v
, we then also have
H^{i}(X,F(m-i))=0
for all
i>0
(
H^{i}(X,Q(m-i))
is automatically zero since
Q
is 0-dimensional). By
[
HL
, Lem. 1.7.6]
, we deduce the required boundedness for
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pairs.
∎

The proof of Lemma
3.5
also proves the following lemma, which will be used in the proof of Proposition
3.17
in the next subsection.

Lemma 3.6
.

The collection of sheaves

\{F\in{\curly Coh}(X,v)(\mathbb{C}):\text{$\exists s\in\Gamma(X,F)$ such that $\operatorname{coker}(\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F)\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\leq 0}(X)$}\}

for fixed
X
and
v
is bounded.

Lastly, we show that the
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
-stability conditions are
open
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(F,s)
be a
T
-flat family of pairs on
X
parametrized by a scheme
T
of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
. Then

\{t\in T(\mathbb{C}):\text{the fibre $(F_{t},s_{t})$ of $(F,s)$ is $\mathrm{PT}_{q}$-stable}\}

is a Zariski open subset of
T(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We first show that the condition

Q_{t}:=\operatorname{coker}(\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s_{t}}F_{t})\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\leq q}(X)

is an open condition.
By
[
Gro2
, Prop. 1.2(i)]
, the set
\{P_{Q_{t}}:t\in T(\mathbb{C})\}
of Hilbert polynomials is finite. Hence the result follows from upper semi-continuity of Hilbert polynomials of non-flat families of coherent sheaves.

Now we consider the second condition

F_{t}\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\geq q+1}(X).

Firstly, if
q=-1
, then this condition is always satisfied. Secondly, if
q=1
, this condition is open by the openness of purity
[
HL
, Prop. 2.3.1]
. Finally, assume that
q=0
. We show that the set of the Hilbert polynomials

(16)

\{P_{{F_{t}}/{T_{0}(F_{t})}}:t\in T(\mathbb{C})\}

is finite. It suffices to show that
\chi(T_{0}(F_{t}))
is bounded above. Note that the short exact sequence
0\to T_{0}(F_{t})\to F_{t}\to F_{t}/T_{0}(F_{t})\to 0

induces a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\curly Ext^{n}_{X}(F_{t}/T_{0}(F_{t}),K_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\curly Ext^{n}_{X}(F_{t},K_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\curly Ext^{n}_{X}(T_{0}(F_{t}),K_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

of 0-dimensional sheaves by
[
HL
, Prop. 1.1.6]
. Then we have

\chi(T_{0}(F_{t}))=\chi(\curly Ext^{n}_{X}(T_{0}(F_{t}),K_{X}))\leq\chi(\curly Ext^{n}_{X}(F_{t},K_{X}))\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{X}(F_{t},K_{X})

where
\pi^{T}:X\times T\to T
is the projection map,
i_{t}:\{t\}\hookrightarrow T
is the inclusion map, and the last inequality follows from the local-to-global spectral sequence. Since
\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{X}(F_{t},K_{X})
are the fibres of the coherent sheaf
\curly Ext^{n}_{\pi^{T}}(F,K_{X})
on a scheme
T
of finite type, their dimensions are bounded above.

Since, for any
t\in T(\mathbb{C})
, the relative Quot schemes

{\curly Quot}(X\times T/T,F,P_{F_{t}/T_{0}(F_{t})})\to T

are proper over
T
, their images are closed. The claim follows from finiteness of (
16
).
∎

Proposition
3.3
follows directly from the above results.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

By Lemma
3.7
, we can form
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
as an open substack of
{\curly Pair}(X,v)
. Then Lemma
3.4
proves that
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
is an algebraic space and Lemma
3.5
proves that
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
is of finite type.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

By
[
Gro2
,
Pot1
]
, the moduli spaces
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs are projective schemes for
q=-1
and
q=1
. In the next subsection, we will show that the space
{\curly Pair}^{(0)}(X,v)
is also a projective scheme.

We end this subsection with a remark on the natural derived enhancement of the moduli stack of pairs. This derived structure will not be used in the rest of the paper.

Remark 3.9
.

There is a derived stack
R\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)
whose classical truncation is
\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)
and the tangent complex at a point
(F,s)
is

\mathbb{T}_{R\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)}|_{(F,s)}=R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F)

where
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F]
. We sketch the construction here. Recall that there exists a derived moduli stack
R{\curly Coh}(X)
of coherent sheaves as an open substack of the derived moduli stack
R{\curly Perf}(X)
of perfect complexes. Let
\mathbb{F}
denote the universal family on
R{\curly Coh}(X)
and let
\pi:R{\curly Coh}(X)\times X\to R{\curly Coh}(X)
denote the projection map. Define the derived moduli stack of pairs as the total space

R\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X):=\mathrm{Tot}_{R{\curly Coh}(X)}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})

of the perfect complex
R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F}
on
R{\curly Coh}(X)
.
Moreover, the derived loop stack

\mathcal{L}_{R\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X),(F,s)}:=\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})\times^{h}_{(F,s),R\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X),(F,s)}\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})

is an open substack of the fibre of the map between derived linear stacks

\mathrm{Tot}(R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(F,F))\xrightarrow{s}\mathrm{Tot}(R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{X},F))

at the point
s\in\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{X},F)
. From the distinguished triangle

\textstyle{R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},F)[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(F,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{X},F)}

the cotangent complex can be computed as

\mathbb{T}_{R\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X)}|_{(F,s)}\cong\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{R\widetilde{{\curly Pair}}(X),(F,s)}}|_{0}[1]\cong R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},F).

3.2.
Geometric invariant theory

In this subsection, we construct moduli spaces of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs as projective schemes via geometric invariant theory. Although we largely follow
[
Pot1
]
, our arguments differ since the underlying sheaves
F
of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
-stable pairs
(F,s)
are not
pure
for
q=-1,0
. Our approach is inspired by
[
ST
]
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety and
v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
.
Then there exists a projective scheme
\mathcal{M}
, an action of
\mathrm{PGL}_{N}
on
\mathcal{M}
for some
N
, and an
\mathrm{SL}_{N}
-equivariant ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{(q)}\in\mathrm{Pic}^{\mathrm{SL}_{N}}(\mathcal{M})_{\mathbb{Q}}
for each
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
such that

{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)=[\mathcal{M}^{st}(\mathcal{L}^{(q)})/\mathrm{PGL}_{N}]=[\mathcal{M}^{ss}(\mathcal{L}^{(q)})/\mathrm{PGL}_{N}].

In particular, the moduli spaces
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
are the GIT quotients
\mathcal{M}/\!/_{\mathcal{L}^{(q)}}\mathrm{SL}_{N}
and hence projective schemes.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.10
. By the boundedness result in Lemma
3.5
, we have
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)\subseteq{\curly Pair}(X,v)_{m}
for sufficiently large
m
. Denoting the Hilbert polynomial determined by
v
by
P_{v}(t)
, we then define

V:=\mathbb{C}^{P_{v}(m)},\quad R_{m}:=\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}(m)).

We use the notation from subsection
3.1
(e.g., the Quot scheme
\mathcal{Q}
in (
11
), the universal family (
12
), and the open subscheme
\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}
in (
13
)). Moreover, by Proposition
3.2
, we have a presentation of the moduli stack as a global quotient stack,

{\curly Pair}(X,v)_{m}=[\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})/\mathrm{PGL}(V)].

We would like to use geometric invariant theory on
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})
. Thus we need to consider a
\mathrm{PGL}(V)
-equivariant compactification of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})
and linearizations on it.

We define the
parameter space

\mathcal{M}
as follows:

Definition 3.11
.

Define a projective scheme

\mathcal{M}:=\overline{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}\times\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})

as the closure of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})
in
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})
with respect to the embedding

(17)

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}(\mathbb{F}(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}\otimes V\otimes R_{m}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}\times\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*}).}

Here the first map in (
17
) is the closed embedding
induced by the canonical map

\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}(m))\otimes R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F}\to R\pi_{*}(\mathbb{F}(m)),

the second map in (
17
) is an isomorphism induced by the canonical map (
14
), and the last map in (
17
) is the open embedding induced by
\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
.

Remark 3.12
.

The parameter space
\mathcal{M}
in this paper is larger than the parameter space of Le Potier in
[
Pot1
,
Pot2
]
. Le Potier considered
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{pure}}\times\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})
where
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{pure}}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
is the closure of the open subscheme
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{pure}}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
. The difference arises from the fact that we want to realize all three moduli spaces
{\curly Pair}^{(q)}(X,v)
for
q=-1,0,1
, as GIT quotients of
the same
scheme
\mathcal{M}
by the same group
\mathrm{PGL}(V)
(with respect to different linearizations).

We consider the
linearizations

\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}
on
\mathcal{M}
defined as follows:

Definition 3.13
.

For a sufficiently large
l
, consider the standard Grothendieck embedding into the Grassmannian

\mathcal{Q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}:={\curly Grass}(V\otimes R_{l-m},P_{v}(l)):(F,\rho)\mapsto(V\otimes R_{l-m}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma(X,F(l))).

Fix a polynomial
g(t)\in\mathbb{Q}[t]
with positive leading coefficient. Define the
\mathrm{SL}(V)
-equivariant very ample line bundle

\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{G}}(1)|_{\mathcal{Q}}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})}(g(l))\in\mathrm{Pic}^{\mathrm{SL}(V)}(\mathcal{M})_{\mathbb{Q}},

where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{G}}(1)
is the polarization on
\mathcal{G}
induced by the standard Plücker embedding of
\mathcal{G}
.

We describe the
\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}
-stable points via the Hilbert-Mumford criterion.

Lemma 3.14
.

A point
(F,\rho,s)

in
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})
is
\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}
-semistable if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(1)

for all non-zero proper subspaces
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
, we have

(18)

\frac{P_{F^{\prime}}(l)}{\dim(V^{\prime})}\geq\frac{P_{F}(l)-g(l)}{\dim(V)}

where
F^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))\subseteq F
, and

(2)

for all non-zero proper subspaces
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
with
s\in V^{\prime}\otimes R_{m}^{*}
, we have

(19)

\frac{P_{F^{\prime}}(l)-g(l)}{\dim(V^{\prime})}\geq\frac{P_{F}(l)-g(l)}{\dim(V)},

where again
F^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))\subseteq F
.

Moreover,
(F,\rho,s)
is
\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}
-stable if and only if the above two inequalities hold strictly.

Proof.

This is standard by
[
HL
, Lem. 4.4.3, Lem. 4.4.4]
(see also
[
ST
, Lem. 3.12]
). For completeness, we briefly go through the argument.

Note that
\mathcal{Q}
has a right
\mathrm{SL}(V)
-action and
\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})
has a left
\mathrm{SL}(V)
-action. Here we will use the left-action convention, i.e.,
\phi\cdot(F,\rho)=(F,\rho\circ\phi^{-1})
for
\phi\in\mathrm{SL}(V)
.

Choose a 1-parameter torus
\lambda:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathrm{SL}(V)
. Equivalently, choose a weight decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i},

where the determinant
1
condition implies
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}i\dim(V_{i})=0
. Let
V_{\leq i}:=\bigoplus_{j\leq i}V_{j}
,
F_{\leq i}:=\rho(V_{\leq i}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))
,
F_{i}:=\frac{F_{\leq i}}{F_{\leq i-1}}
, and
\rho_{i}:V_{i}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m)\twoheadrightarrow F_{i}
be the induced surjective map. Let
s=\sum_{i}s_{i}
such that
s_{i}\in V_{i}\otimes R_{m}^{*}
and let
i_{\max}
be the maximal integer
i
such that
s_{i}\neq 0
.

As in
[
HL
, Lem. 4.4.3]
, the limit point is

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lambda(t)\cdot(F,\rho,s)=(\bigoplus_{i}F_{i},\bigoplus_{i}\rho_{i},s_{i_{\mathrm{max}}}).

Since the fibre of the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}
over a point
(F,\rho,s)
is given as

\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}|_{(F,\rho,s)}=\det(H^{0}(X,F(l)))\otimes\mathbb{C}\langle s\rangle^{\otimes-g(l)}

where
\mathbb{C}\langle s\rangle\subseteq V\otimes R_{m}^{*}
is the subspace generated by
s
, we can easily compute the Hilbert-Mumford weight

\displaystyle\mu^{\lambda^{-1}}_{\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}}(F,\rho,s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left(-i\cdot P_{F_{i}}(l)+i_{\mathrm{max}}\cdot g(l)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left(\left(P_{F_{\leq i}}(l)-\dim(\Gamma_{\leq i})\cdot g(l)\right)-\frac{\dim(V_{\leq i})}{\dim(V)}\cdot(P_{F}(l)-g(l))\right)

where
\Gamma_{\leq i}:=\mathbb{C}\langle s\rangle\cap(V_{\leq i}\otimes R_{m}^{*})
.

By
[
MFK
, Thm. 2.1]
, the point
(F,\rho,s)
is semistable if and only if

\frac{P_{F^{\prime}}(l)-\dim(\Gamma^{\prime})\cdot g(l)}{\dim(V^{\prime})}\geq\frac{P_{F}(l)-g(l)}{\dim(V)}

for all proper subspaces
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
, where
F^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))
and
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\mathbb{C}\langle s\rangle\cap(V^{\prime}\otimes R_{m}^{*})
. This completes the proof.
∎

We will focus on the following three types of linearizations:

Definition 3.15
.

Recall that
P_{v}(t)\in\mathbb{Q}[t]
is the Hilbert polynomial associated to
v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
.

(
\mathrm{DT}
)

For any rational number
a>0
, we define a polynomial

g^{(-1)}(t):=P_{v}(t)+a\cdot t^{2}.

(
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
)

For any rational number
0<\epsilon<1
, we define a polynomial

g^{(0)}(t):=P_{v}(t)-\epsilon\cdot t.

(
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
)

For any rational number
0<\delta<\tfrac{1}{2}
, we define a polynomial

g^{(1)}(t):=P_{v}(t)-\delta\cdot t^{2}.

For each integer
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
, we define a linearization

\mathcal{L}^{(q)}:=\mathcal{L}_{g^{(q)}(t),l}

for sufficiently large
l
.

Remark 3.16
.

We list the linearizations previously considered in the literature
[
Pot1
,
Pot2
,
ST
]
.

(1)

In
[
Pot1
]
, Le Potier considered
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{G}}(c)\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})}(\frac{l^{d}}{d!})
for
c>1
.

(2)

In
[
Pot2
]
, Le Potier considered
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{G}}(1+\frac{\alpha(m)}{P_{v}(m)})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})}(P_{v}(l))
for
\alpha(t)\in\mathbb{Q}[t]
.

(3)

In
[
ST
]
, Stoppa-Thomas considered
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{G}}(c)\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})}(l)
for
c>0
(for 1-dimensional pairs).

The linearizations
\mathcal{L}_{g(t),l}
in this paper are 2-dimensional versions of the linearizations in
[
ST
]
, which contain the linearizations in
[
Pot1
]
as a special case.
However our parameter space is larger than the one in
[
Pot1
]
, as explained in Remark
3.12
.

We now compare GIT stability for the line bundles
\mathcal{L}^{(q)}
in Definition
3.15
and
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
-stability introduced in Definition
2.1
.

Proposition 3.17
.

There exists an integer
M
which only depends on
X
and
v
, and integers
L_{m}
which depend on
X
,
v
, and
m
such that for any
m\geq M
,
l\geq L_{m}
, and
(F,\rho,s)\in\mathcal{M}
, we have the following:

(1)

(F,\rho,s)
is
\mathcal{L}^{(q)}
-semistable
\implies

(F,\rho)\in\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}
and
(F,s)
is
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
-stable;

(2)

(F,\rho)\in\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}
and
(F,s)
is
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
-stable
\implies

(F,\rho,s)
is
\mathcal{L}^{(q)}
-stable.

We will consider the three stability conditions separately. We start with
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
-stability.

Proof of Proposition
3.17
for
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
-stability.

(1) Assume that
(F,\rho,s)
is
\mathcal{L}^{(0)}
-semistable.
We first claim that the induced map

\Gamma(\rho(m)):V\to\Gamma(X,F(m))

is injective. Let
V^{\prime}
be the kernel of the above map
\Gamma(\rho(m))
. Then the induced sheaf
F^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))\subseteq F
is zero. If
V^{\prime}\neq 0
, then the
\mathcal{L}^{(0)}
-semistability of
(F,\rho,s)
implies an inequality

\frac{0}{\dim(V^{\prime})}\geq\frac{\epsilon\cdot l}{\dim(V)}

by Lemma
3.14
(1). This leads to a contradiction for
l>0
. Hence
V^{\prime}=0
.

We claim that
(F,s)\in{\curly Pair}(X,v)
. More precisely, there exists a section
\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F
that fits into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\otimes R_{m}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(\rho(m))}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*}}

determined by the dotted arrow. Indeed, consider the open subscheme

\mathcal{Q}^{\star}:=\{(F,\rho)\in\mathcal{Q}:V\xrightarrow{\Gamma(\rho(m))}\Gamma(X,F(m))\text{ is injective}\}

of
\mathcal{Q}
. Note that the restriction
\mathcal{M}|_{\mathcal{Q}^{\star}}
of
\mathcal{M}
is the closure of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})
in
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}\times\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})
. Hence we can form a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{M}|_{\mathcal{Q}^{\star}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\star}}(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}^{\star}\otimes\mathbb{P}(V\otimes R_{m}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\star}}(R\pi_{*}(\mathbb{F}(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*})}

of closed embeddings of projective schemes over
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
. By the result in the previous paragraph, we have
(F,\rho,s)\in\mathcal{M}|_{\mathcal{Q}^{\star}}
, which proves the claim.

Next, we claim that
Q:=\operatorname{coker}(\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F)\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\leq 0}(X)
. Let

V^{\prime}:=V\cap\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z}(m))\quad\text{in}\quad\Gamma(X,F(m)),

where
\mathcal{O}_{Z}:=\operatorname{im}(\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F)
. Then the induced sheaf
F^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))\subseteq F
is contained in
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\subseteq F
. Note that we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{V^{\prime}\otimes R_{m}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z}(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{V\otimes R_{m}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho(m)}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the left square is cartesian. Hence we have
s\in V^{\prime}\otimes R_{m}^{*}
. By Lemma
3.14
(2), we have inequalities

(20)

\frac{P_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}(l)-g^{(0)}(l)}{\dim(V^{\prime})}\geq\frac{P_{F^{\prime}}(l)-g^{(0)}(l)}{\dim(V^{\prime})}\geq\frac{\epsilon\cdot l}{\dim(V)},

for sufficiently large
l
, where the two collections

\{\mathcal{O}_{Z}:=\operatorname{im}(\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F)\}\{F^{\prime}:=\operatorname{im}(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m)\xrightarrow{\rho}F)\}

for fixed
X
,
v
,
m
are bounded by
[
Gro2
, Prop. 1.2(i)]
. Hence the set
\{P_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}(t)\}
of Hilbert polynomials is finite. Since the leading coefficient of the left-hand side of (
20
), as a polynomial in
l
, should be positive, we can take
l
sufficiently large and deduce
\alpha_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\alpha_{2}(F)
and
\alpha_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})>\alpha_{1}(F)-\epsilon
.
Since
\epsilon<1
, we have
\alpha_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\alpha_{1}(F)
, and thus
Q
is 0-dimensional.

We now show that
(F,\rho)\in\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}
. By Lemma
3.6
, the collection of coherent sheaves
F
with
\operatorname{ch}(F)=v
and
\operatorname{coker}(s)\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\leq 0}(X)
is bounded for fixed
X,v
.
Hence we have
H^{>0}(X,F(m))=0
for sufficiently large
m
, as desired.

We finally claim that
F\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\geq 1}(X)
. Let
T_{0}(F)\subseteq F
be the 0-dimensional torsion sheaf and let

V^{\prime}:=\Gamma(X,T_{0}(F)(m))\subseteq\Gamma(X,F(m))\xleftarrow{\cong}V,

where the last isomorphism follows from the result in the previous paragraph. Then the induced sheaf
F^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))\subseteq F
is
T_{0}(F)
since the 0-dimensional sheaf
T_{0}(F)
is globally generated. If
V^{\prime}\neq 0
, then we have an inequality

1=\frac{\chi(T_{0}(F))}{\chi(T_{0}(F)(m))}=\frac{\chi(F^{\prime})}{\dim(V^{\prime})}\geq\frac{\epsilon\cdot l}{\dim(V)}

by Lemma
3.14
(1). This leads to contradiction for big enough
l
. Therefore
V^{\prime}=0
and
F^{\prime}=T_{0}(F)=0
.

(2) Assume that
(F,\rho)\in\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}
and
(F,s)
is
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
-stable. We will prove the
\mathcal{L}^{(0)}
-stability of
(F,\rho,s)
through Lemma
3.14
. Choose a proper non-zero subspace
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
and let
F^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m))\subseteq F
be the induced subsheaf. Then we have a commutative square

(21)

\textstyle{V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F^{\prime}(m))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F(m)).}

We first prove the inequality (
18
) in Lemma
3.14
(1). Note that the collection

\{F^{\prime}=\operatorname{im}(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-m)\xrightarrow{\rho}F):(F,\rho)\in\mathcal{Q}^{\circ}V^{\prime}\subseteq V\}

for fixed
X,v,m
is bounded by
[
Gro2
, Prop. 1.2.(i)]
. Hence the set
\{P_{F^{\prime}}(t)\}
of the corresponding Hilbert polynomials is finite. Since
F^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\geq 1}(X)
by
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
-stability and
\chi(F^{\prime})
has a lower bound, independent of the choices of
F
,
V^{\prime}
,
l
, we have an inequality

\frac{P_{F^{\prime}}(l)}{\dim(V^{\prime})}>\frac{l+\chi(F^{\prime})}{\dim(V)}>\frac{\epsilon\cdot l}{\dim(V)}

for sufficiently large
l
. This proves (
18
).

We then prove the second inequality (
19
) in Lemma
3.14
(2) when
s\in V^{\prime}\otimes R_{m}^{*}
. Indeed, consider the canonical commutative diagram

(22)

\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F/F^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F^{\prime}(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,F/F^{\prime}(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*}}

induced by the short exact sequence
0\to F^{\prime}\to F\to F/F^{\prime}\to 0
. By (
21
), we have
s\in\Gamma(X,F^{\prime}(m))\otimes R_{m}^{*}
.
By a diagram chasing argument, we can deduce that the left square in (
22
) is cartesian. Hence
s\in\Gamma(X,F^{\prime})
and we have
\mathcal{O}_{Z}:=\operatorname{im}(s:\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F)\subseteq F^{\prime}
as subsheaves of
F
. Since
(F,s)
is
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
-stable, we have
\alpha_{2}(F^{\prime})=\alpha_{2}(F)
and
\alpha_{1}(F^{\prime})=\alpha_{1}(F)
. Since
\chi(F^{\prime})
has a lower bound, independent of the choices of
F
,
V^{\prime}
,
l
, we have an inequality

\frac{\epsilon\cdot l+\chi(F^{\prime})-\chi(F)}{\dim(V^{\prime})}>\frac{\epsilon\cdot l}{\dim(V)}

for sufficiently large
l
. This proves (
19
).
∎

By
[
Gro2
,
Pot1
]
, the moduli spaces
{\curly Pair}^{(-1)}(X,v)
and
{\curly Pair}^{(1)}(X,v)
are projective schemes (see Remark
3.8
). We will use these results to prove Proposition
3.17
for the
\mathrm{DT}
-stability and the
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
-stability.
