, Lem. 5.5.3]
), we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{2p}(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{2p}(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathrm{an}},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\mathrm{an}}}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2p}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{an}},\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{2p}(X^{\mathrm{an}},\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{2p}(\mathcal{X},\Omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{2p}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{an}},\Omega_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{an}}/\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{an}}}^{\bullet})}
\textstyle{H^{2p}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{an}},A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{2p}(X^{\mathrm{an}},A)}

where the last vertical map is induced by the canonical map of constant sheaves
\mathbb{C}\to A
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
.
Hence the image of the vertical map consists of the horizontal sections (see Remark
3.31
), which proves the claim.

(3)

We finally claim that the restriction
\widetilde{\operatorname{ch}_{p}}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
to the fibre of a point
b\in\mathcal{B}
is the usual Chern character. Indeed, we may assume that
\mathcal{B}=\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})
since Chern characters are compatible with base change. Then the claim follows from
[
Wei3
, Prop. 3.8.1]
.

This completes the proof.
∎

4.
Virtual cycles

In this section, we construct reduced virtual cycles for the moduli spaces of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs on Calabi-Yau 4-folds. We use Kiem-Li’s cosection localization technique
[
KL
,
KiPa2
]
to reduce the Oh-Thomas virtual cycles
[
OT1
]
on the moduli spaces.
The semi-regularity map of Buchweitz-Flenner
[
BuFl1
]
will provide the necessary cosections.

Throughout this section, we fix a (smooth projective) Calabi-Yau 4-fold
X
and a Calabi-Yau 4-form
\omega:\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Omega^{4}_{X}
.

4.1.
Non-reduced virtual cycles

In this preliminary subsection, we construct
non-reduced
virtual cycles for the moduli spaces of stable pairs.
We review Oh-Thomas’s construction of virtual cycles in
[
OT1
]
for the reader’s convenience. Instead of following
[
OT1
]
directly, we will use the setup introduced in
[
Par1
]
.

Let
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
be the moduli space of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs
(F,s)
on
X
satisfying

\operatorname{ch}(F)=v=\left(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X)\right)\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q}).

By Theorem
3.19
, we have an open embedding

\curly P\hookrightarrow{\curly Perf}(X,v)_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathrm{spl}}

into the moduli space of perfect complexes with fixed trivial determinant. Thus, by
[
OT1
]
, we can construct an Oh-Thomas virtual cycle on
\curly P
.

Theorem 4.1

(
[
OT1
]
)
.

There exists a virtual cycle

\left[\curly P\right]^{\mathrm{vir}}\in A_{\mathrm{vd}}\left(\curly P\right)

of virtual dimension

\mathrm{vd}=n-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2},

which depends
11
11

11

A different choice of orientation changes the
signs
of the virtual cycles on the connected components.
on the choice of an orientation (
53
) below.

We call the virtual cycle
[\curly P]^{\mathrm{vir}}
in Theorem
4.1
the
non-reduced virtual cycle
. We use this terminology since we will construct a canonical
reduced virtual cycle

[\curly P]^{\mathrm{red}}
for any Calabi-Yau 4-fold
X
and a surface class
\gamma
in the next subsection (see Theorem
4.5
).

There are three key ingredients for defining the Oh-Thomas virtual cycle (in the language of
[
Par1
]
):

(1)

Symmetric obstruction theory.

The moduli space
\curly P
has a (3-term)
symmetric obstruction theory

(52)

\phi:\mathbb{E}:=R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[3]\xrightarrow{\operatorname{At}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P},\quad\theta:\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[2]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SD}}\mathbb{E}

induced by the Atiyah class
\operatorname{At}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
of the universal complex
\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
and the relative Serre duality for the projection map
\pi:\curly P\times X\to\curly P
. Here
\phi
and
\theta
satisfy the following properties:

(a)

\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}
is an
obstruction theory
,
[
BF
]
i.e.,
h^{0}(\phi)
is bijective and
h^{-1}(\phi)
is surjective;

(b)

\mathbb{E}
is a
symmetric complex
(of tor-amplitude
[-2,0]
), i.e.,
\theta
is an isomorphism and
\theta=\theta^{\vee}[2]
.

The symmetric obstruction theory (
52
) is constructed by Huybrechts-Thomas
[
HT
]
.
12
12

12

Alternatively, we can also use the derived enhancement of
{\curly Perf}(X)^{\mathrm{spl}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}
in
[
ToVa
,
STV
]
.

(2)

Orientation.

There exists an
orientation
for the virtual cotangent complex
\mathbb{E}
, i.e., an isomorphism of line bundles

(53)

o:\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\xrightarrow{\cong}\det(\mathbb{E})

such that
o^{2}=(-1)^{\frac{(2\mathrm{vd})(2\mathrm{vd}-1)}{2}}\cdot\det(\theta):\det(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})\to\det(\mathbb{E})
. The existence of an orientation was shown by Cao-Gross-Joyce
[
CGJ
]
.

(3)

Isotropic condition.
The intrinsic normal cone
\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}
is
isotropic
in the virtual normal cone
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}):=h^{1}/h^{0}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})
with respect to the canonical quadratic function
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
(defined in Remark
4.2
below), i.e., the restriction

\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}|_{\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}}:\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\curly P}

vanishes. This isotropic condition can be shown by the Darboux theorem of Brav-Bussi-Joyce
[
BBJ
]
for the
(-2)
-shifted symplectic derived enhancement
[
PTVV
,
STV
]
. Indeed, by
[
Par1
, Lem. 1.11]
, the arguments in
[
OT1
, Prop. 4.3]
proves the isotropic condition.
13
13

13

One does not need the full proof of
[
OT1
, Prop. 4.3]
, but only the part involving MacPherson’s graph construction
[
Ful
, Rem. 5.11]
.

We recall the definition of the canonical quadratic function
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}
from
[
Par1
]
.

Remark 4.2
.

Given a symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
on a quasi-projective scheme
\mathscr{X}
, there is a canonical quadratic function

\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathscr{X}}

induced by the symmetric form of
\mathbb{E}
. Indeed, if
\mathbb{E}=[B\to E\to B^{\vee}]
is a symmetric resolution, then
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})=[C(D)/B]
for
D=\operatorname{coker}(B\to E)
, and the restriction
\mathfrak{q}_{E}|_{C(D)}:C(D)\hookrightarrow E\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{q}_{E}}\mathbb{A}^{1}
of the quadratic function
\mathfrak{q}_{E}
of
E
is
B
-invariant. The quadratic function
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}
is defined via descent of
\mathfrak{q}_{E}|_{C(D)}
, which is independent of the choice of the symmetric resolution. See
[
Par1
, Prop. 1.7]
for details.

Note that from the above data, we can form a commutative diagram

(54)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\curly P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\curly P}}

where
\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
is the
quadratic cone stack
associated to the symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
, defined as the zero locus of the quadratic function
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}
on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
.

We can construct the Oh-Thomas virtual cycle
[\curly P]^{\mathrm{vir}}
from the closed embedding
\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
in (
54
).

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We first construct a
square root Gysin pullback

\sqrt{0^{!}_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}}:A_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}))\to A_{*}(\curly P)

for the zero section
0_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}:\curly P\to\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
. Note that we can find a symmetric resolution

(55)

\mathbb{E}\cong\left[B\xrightarrow{d}E\cong E^{\vee}\xrightarrow{d^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\right]

for some special orthogonal bundle
E
and a vector bundle
B
. Indeed, this can be shown by the proof of
[
OT1
, Prop. 4.1]
, see also
[
Par1
, Prop. 1.3]
.
Here an orientation of
\mathbb{E}
induces an orientation of
E
via the canonical isomorphism

\det(\mathbb{E})\cong\det(B^{\vee})\otimes\det(E^{\vee})^{\vee}\otimes\det(B)\cong\det(E)

of line bundles. By considering the stupid truncation

\left[0\to E^{\vee}\xrightarrow{d^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\right]\to\left[B\xrightarrow{d}E\cong E^{\vee}\xrightarrow{d^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\right],

we can form a fibre diagram

(56)

\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[E/B]}

for some cone
Q
. The horizontal maps in (
56
) are closed embeddings and the vertical maps in (
56
) are
B
-torsors. Then
Q
is isotropic subcone of
E
since

\mathfrak{q}_{E}|_{Q}=\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})}|_{Q}=0

by
[
Par1
, Prop. 1.7(3)]
.
Consider a factorization

\textstyle{E|_{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\curly P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{0_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}

of the zero section
0_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}
, where
\tau
is the tautological section induced by the embedding
Q\hookrightarrow E
in (
56
). Note that the zero section
0_{Q}:\curly P\hookrightarrow Q
is the zero locus of the tautological section
\tau\in\Gamma(Q,E|_{Q})
. We define the square root Gysin pullback as the composition

(57)

\sqrt{0^{!}_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}}:=\sqrt{e}(E|_{Q},\tau)\circ r^{*}:A_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}))\xrightarrow{}A_{*+b}(Q)\xrightarrow{}A_{*+b-\frac{1}{2}e}(\curly P)

where
b:=\operatorname{rk}(B)
,
e:=\operatorname{rk}(E)
,
\mathrm{vd}=b-\frac{1}{2}e
, and
\sqrt{e}(E|_{Q},\tau)
denotes Oh-Thomas’s localization
[
OT1
, Def. 3.2]
of Edidin-Graham’s square root Euler class
[
EG1
]
.
14
14

14

See also
[
KiPa2
, Def. 4.1]
for an alternative construction of the localized square root Euler class
\sqrt{e}(E|_{C},\tau)
using the blowup method.

The arguments in
[
OT1
, pp. 31–34]
show that the square root Gysin pullback in (
57
) is independent of the choice of the symmetric resolution (
55
).

We then define the Oh-Thomas virtual cycle as

(58)

\left[\curly P\right]^{\mathrm{vir}}:=\sqrt{0^{!}_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}}\left[\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}\right]\in A_{\mathrm{vd}}\left(\curly P\right)

where

[\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}]\in A_{0}(\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}))

is the fundamental class of the intrinsic normal cone with respect to the closed embedding
\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
in (
54
).

We can easily compute the virtual dimension via the Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula
[
Ful
, Cor. 15.2.1]
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle 2\cdot\mathrm{vd}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rank}(R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[1])

\displaystyle=-\int_{X}\operatorname{ch}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})^{\vee}\operatorname{ch}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})\mathrm{td}(X)+\int_{X}\mathrm{td}(X)

\displaystyle=-(-2n+2\mathrm{td}_{2}(X)\gamma+\mathrm{td}_{4}(X)-2\mathrm{td}_{2}(X)\gamma+\gamma^{2})+\mathrm{td}_{4}(X)

\displaystyle=2n-\gamma^{2}.

It completes the proof.
∎

We provide a simple analogy between the above description (
58
) of the Oh-Thomas virtual cycles
[
OT1
]
and the Behrend-Fantechi virtual cycles
[
BF
]
.

Remark 4.3
.

Fix an arbitrary (separated) Deligne-Mumford stack
\mathscr{X}
.

Recall that a perfect obstruction theory
\psi:\mathbb{K}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
of tor-amplitude
[-1,0]
is equivalent to a closed embedding of cone stacks
15
15

15

A morphism of cone stacks is always assumed to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-equivariant as in
[
BF
, Def. 1.8]
.

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{K})
, for some vector bundle stack
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{K})
. In
[
Kre1
]
, Kresch constructed a Gysin pullback

0^{!}_{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{K})}:A_{*}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{K}))\to A_{*}(\mathscr{X})

for any perfect complex
\mathbb{K}
of tor-amplitude
[-1,0]
. In
[
BF
,
Kre1
]
, the Behrend-Fantechi virtual cycle for a closed embedding
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{K})
was defined as

[\mathscr{X}]^{\mathrm{vir}}_{BF}:=0^{!}_{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{K})}[\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}].

Analogously, a symmetric obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
of tor-amplitude
[-2,0
] satisfying the isotropic condition is equivalent to a closed embedding of cone stacks
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
, for some quadratic cone stack
\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
associated to a symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
. Note that we can define the square root Gysin pullback

\sqrt{0^{!}_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}}:A_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}))\to A_{*}(\mathscr{X})

for any symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
of tor-amplitude
[-2,0]
and an orientation
\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\det(\mathbb{E})
as in the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
16
16

16

The proof of Theorem
4.1
works when
\mathscr{X}
is a quasi-projective scheme. Using the Kimura sequence for Artin stacks
[
BP
]
, this can be generalized to the case when
\mathscr{X}
is a separated Deligne-Mumford stack, see
[
Par1
, A]
.
Thus the Oh-Thomas virtual cycle for a closed embedding
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
and an orientation can be defined as

[\mathscr{X}]^{\mathrm{vir}}_{OT}:=\sqrt{0^{!}_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})}}[\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}].

4.2.
Reduced virtual cycles

Recall that the standard curve counting invariants (GW/PT) on K3 surfaces vanish due to the following two reasons:

(1)

There exists a surjective cosection
[
KL
]
;

(2)

the codimension of the Noether-Lefschetz locus is positive
[
KT1
,
MPT
]
.

To obtain non-zero invariants, one should consider
reduced
virtual cycles.

An analogous phenomenon occurs when we want to count surfaces on Calabi-Yau 4-folds. Given a surface class
\gamma
, there are as many natural cosections as the codimension
\rho_{\gamma}
of the Hodge locus. Thus in a generic situation, the non-reduced virtual cycle in Theorem
4.1
vanishes and we want to construct a
reduced
virtual cycle. This can be achieved by the DT4 version of the cosection localization technique developed in
[
KiPa2
]
. In this subsection, we present the main results on the reduced virtual cycles and we provide the proofs in the subsequent subsections.

We first introduce an important bilinear form
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
on
H^{1}(X,T_{X})
associated to each surface class
\gamma
on
X
. We also fix some notation.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\gamma\in H^{2}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})
be a (2,2)-class on
X
.

(1)

We define a symmetric bilinear form
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
on
H^{1}(X,T_{X})
as

\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}:H^{1}(X,T_{X})\otimes H^{1}(X,T_{X})\to\mathbb{C},\quad\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{2}\mapsto\int_{X}\iota_{\xi_{1}}\iota_{\xi_{2}}\gamma\cup\omega

where
\iota_{\xi_{1}}
and
\iota_{\xi_{2}}
denote the contraction maps and
\omega
is the (chosen) Calabi-Yau 4-form.
17
17

17

For
\xi_{1}\in H^{i}(X,T_{X})
and
\xi_{2}\in H^{j}(X,T_{X})
, we have
\iota_{\xi_{2}}\iota_{\xi_{1}}=(-1)^{(1+i)(1+j)}\iota_{\xi_{1}}\iota_{\xi_{2}}
(see for example
[
BBJ
, Lem. 2.5]
).

(2)

We denote the rank of the bilinear form
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
by

\rho_{\gamma}:=\mathrm{rank}(\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}).

(3)

For the induced non-degenerate quadratic space of dimension
\rho_{\gamma}
, we write

H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma}:=\frac{H^{1}(X,T_{X})}{\ker(\mathsf{B}_{\gamma})}.

The bilinear form
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
is closely related to the Hodge theory of the (2,2)-class

\gamma\in H^{2}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})\subseteq H^{4}(X,\mathbb{C}).

Essentially, the rank
\rho_{\gamma}
is equal to the codimension of the Hodge locus of
\gamma

[
Blo
]
(see footnote (
3
)).

We now state our main result.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau 4-fold,
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, and
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
.
Then there exists a canonical reduced virtual cycle

\left[{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\right]^{\mathrm{red}}\in A_{\mathrm{rvd}}\left({\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\right)

of reduced virtual dimension

\mathrm{rvd}=n-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\gamma}

depending on the choices of orientation of the symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
and the quadratic space
H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma}
. Moreover,
[{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)]^{\mathrm{red}}
is deformation invariant in the sense of Theorem
5.1
.

The reduced virtual cycle
[{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)]^{\mathrm{red}}

has a relation to the notion of
semi-regularity
of perfect complexes
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
associated to pairs
[(F,s)]\in{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
(Definition
4.8
(2)).

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau 4-fold,
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, and
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
.
For any
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair
[(F,s)]\in{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
, the associated perfect complex
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}:=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F]
is semi-regular if and only if

{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
is smooth of dimension
\mathrm{rvd}=n-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\gamma}
at
[(F,s)]
.

By Theorem
4.6
, if all point in
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
are semi-regular, then it is smooth and the reduced virtual cycle is equal to the fundamental cycle

\left[{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\right]^{\mathrm{red}}=\left[{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\right]\in A_{\mathrm{rvd}}\left({\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\right).

Remark 4.7
.

The role of
\rho_{\gamma}
in the surface counting theory is an interesting new feature compared to known enumerative geometry. Indeed
\rho_{\gamma}
is not a topological datum of
\gamma\in H^{4}(X)
but rather it is a Hodge theoretic datum of
\gamma\in H^{2,2}(X)
.
In particular rank of
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
is not additive in
\gamma
in general, but it is only sub-additive, i.e.,
\rho_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}\leq\rho_{\gamma_{1}}+\rho_{\gamma_{2}}
. For example
\rho_{\gamma}
remains the same when taking multiples of
\gamma
.

We now explain the main ideas in the proof of Theorem
4.5
. We will consider three different, but interrelated, approaches to the reduced theory.

(1)

Cosection localization.
In subsection
4.4
, we will prove Theorem
4.5
using the
cone reduction
lemma of Kiem-Li
[
KL
]
. The
semi-regularity map
of Buchweitz-Flenner
[
BuFl1
]
will provide the necessary cosections. This cosection localization method is the simplest way to construct the reduced virtual cycle.

(2)

Algebraic twistor family.
In subsection
4.5
, we will construct a
reduced obstruction theory
using the algebraic twistor family introduced by the second-named author and Thomas
[
KT1
]
.
The main idea is to consider an infinitesimal deformation of
X
such that the Hodge locus of
\gamma
is just the origin.
The algebraic twistor family method alone does not provide a reduced virtual cycle without the cone reduction lemma due to the isotropic condition. See Remark
4.22
.

(3)

Reduction by
(-1)
-shifted
1
-forms.
In Appendix
A
, we will construct a
reduced
(-2)
-shifted symplectic derived enhancement
.
The crucial part is that the cosections arising from the semi-regularity map have natural extensions to
(-1)
-shifted
closed

1
-forms.

4.3.
Semi-regularity map

In this subsection, we revisit the
semi-regularity map
of Buchweitz-Flenner
[
BuFl1
]
and show that the key commutative triangle in
[
BuFl1
]
is
symmetric
for Calabi-Yau 4-folds.

We first recall the definition of the semi-regularity map.
18
18

18

Here we consider the fixed-determinant version of the semi-regularity map. For strict Calabi-Yau 4-folds, this is identical to the 2-semi-regularity map in
[
BuFl1
]
. Also we use a different sign convention compared to
[
BuFl1
]
.

Definition 4.8

(cf.
[
Blo
,
BuFl1
]
)
.

Let
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
be a perfect complex on
X
.

(1)

We define the
semi-regularity map
by

(59)

\mathsf{sr}:\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\to H^{3}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}),\quad e\mapsto\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{At}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})\circ e),

where
\operatorname{At}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}):I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\to I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X}[1]
denotes the Atiyah class of
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
.

(2)

We say that
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
is
semi-regular
if
\mathsf{sr}
is injective.

The semi-regularity map connects the obstruction to deforming a perfect complex
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
and its Chern character
\operatorname{ch}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
by the key commutative triangle in
[
BuFl1
, Cor. 4.3]
. For Calabi-Yau 4-folds, this commutative triangle has a
symmetric
description.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
be a perfect complex on
X
such that
\operatorname{ch}_{1}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})=0\in H^{1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})
and
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})=\gamma\in H^{2}(X,\Omega_{X}^{2})
.
Then the obstruction map

(60)

\mathsf{ob}:H^{1}(X,T_{X})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0},\quad\xi\mapsto\iota_{\xi}\operatorname{At}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})

fits into the commutative diagram

(61)

\textstyle{H^{1}(X,T_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ob}}
\scriptstyle{B_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{-}(\gamma)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ob}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{sr}}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{SD}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}}

where the vertical isomorphism
\mathsf{SD}

is induced by the Serre duality pairing

H^{3}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\otimes H^{1}(X,T_{X})\to\mathbb{C},\quad\alpha\otimes\xi\mapsto\int_{X}\iota_{\xi}(\alpha)\cup\omega

and we identified
(\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0})^{\vee}=\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}
via the Yoneda pairing

\mathsf{Y}:\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\otimes\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\to\mathbb{C},\quad u\otimes v\mapsto\int_{X}\operatorname{tr}(u\circ v)\cup\omega.

Proof.

Note that the obstruction map
\mathsf{ob}
in (
60
) is well-defined since

\operatorname{tr}\circ\mathsf{ob}(\xi)=\iota_{\xi}\operatorname{ch}_{1}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})=0.

The commutativity of the three triangles in (
61
) can be shown as follows:

(1)

(top triangle) We have

\mathsf{sr}\circ\mathsf{ob}=\iota_{-}(\gamma)

by
[
BuFl1
, Cor. 4.3]
.

(2)

(left triangle) For any
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in H^{1}(X,T_{X})
, we have

\mathsf{SD}(\iota_{\xi_{1}}(\gamma))(\xi_{2})=\int_{X}\iota_{\xi_{2}}\iota_{\xi_{1}}\gamma\cup\omega=\int_{X}\iota_{\xi_{1}}\iota_{\xi_{2}}\gamma\cup\omega=\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}).

(3)

(right triangle) For any
\xi\in H^{1}(X,T_{X})
and
e\in\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
, we have

\mathsf{ob}^{\vee}(e)(\xi)=\int_{X}\operatorname{tr}(\iota_{\xi}\operatorname{At}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})\circ e)\cup\omega=\int_{X}\iota_{\xi}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{At}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})\circ e)\cup\omega=\mathsf{SD}(\mathsf{sr}(e))(\xi).

This completes the proof.
∎

The commutative triangle (
61
) says that the map of vector spaces

\mathsf{ob}=\mathsf{sr}^{\vee}:\left(H^{1}(X,T_{X}),\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0},\mathsf{Y}\right)

is compatible with the corresponding symmetric bilinear forms. Equivalently, we may write
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{ob}^{*}(\mathsf{Y})
.

In the following subsections, we will develop the reduced theory with respect to a non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
. We provide a simple observation on such non-degenerate subspaces.

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
be a perfect complex on
X
with
\operatorname{ch}_{1}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})=0
and
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})=\gamma
.
Choose a non-degenerate subspace

V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})

with respect to the bilinear form
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
.
Then the restriction

(\mathsf{ob}=\mathsf{sr}^{\vee})|_{V}:\left(V,\,\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}|_{V}\right)\hookrightarrow\left(\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0},\,\mathsf{Y}\right)

is an injective map of non-degenerate quadratic spaces.

Proof.

If
\mathsf{ob}(v)=0
for some
v\in V
, then we have

\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}(v,-)=\int_{X}\operatorname{tr}\big(\mathsf{ob}(v)\circ\mathsf{ob}(-)\big)=0\in H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}.

Thus the non-degeneracy of
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}|_{V}
implies that
v=0
.
∎

For reader’s convenience, we review how the maps
\operatorname{ob}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
in the commutative triangle (
61
) determine the obstructions of deforming the perfect complex
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
and the Hodge class
\gamma\in H^{2}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})
, respectively.
For simplicity, we only consider the first-order deformations here.
We refer to
[
Blo
, Prop. 4.2]
and
[
Ill
, Prop. IV.3.1.5]
(cf.
[
HT
, Cor. 3.4]
) for the general case.

Lemma 4.11
.

Consider a first-order deformation of
X
, i.e., a fibre diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\{o\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}

where
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{A}
is a smooth projective morphism and

\mathcal{A}
is a square zero extension of
\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})
.
Let
o\in\mathcal{A}
be the unique point.

(1)

Given a
(2,2)
-class
\gamma\in H^{2}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})\subseteq H^{4}(X,\mathbb{C})
,
there exists a horizontal section
\widetilde{\gamma}\in F^{2}H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{A})
such that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{o}=\gamma
if and only if the composition

T_{o,\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{KS_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{A}}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}

vanishes.

(2)

Given a perfect complex
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
on
\mathcal{X}
with
\operatorname{ch}_{1}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})=0
,
there exists a perfect complex
\widetilde{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
on
\mathcal{X}
with
\det(\widetilde{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
such that
\widetilde{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{o}\cong I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
if and only if the composition

(62)

T_{o,\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{KS_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{A}}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{ob}}\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}

vanishes.

Proof.

(1) follows directly from
[
Blo
, Prop. 4.2]
.
We would like to deduce (2) from
[
Ill
, Prop. IV.3.1.5]
(or
[
HT
, Cor. 3.4]
). Consider a diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{X}\cong\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{A}}|_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{A}}|_{X}}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{X/\mathcal{A}}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{X/\mathcal{X}}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{X/\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}_{X/\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{A}}|_{X}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{X/\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathbb{L}_{o/\mathcal{A}}|_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}_{o/\mathcal{A}}|_{X}}

where the left two triangles commute with the dotted arrows and the right two triangles commute with the solid arrows. Moreover, the composition of the middle arrows is zero because
\operatorname{At}_{X/\mathcal{A}}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})
factors through
\operatorname{At}_{X}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})
.
Hence the total big square commutes (up to sign). Therefore, the map (
62
) corresponds to the composition

I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{At}_{X/\mathcal{X}}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{X/\mathcal{X}}[1]\to I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{X/\mathcal{X}}[2]=I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{o/\mathcal{A}}[2]

via adjunction.
∎

4.4.
Cosection localization

In this subsection, we construct the
reduced virtual cycle
and prove Theorem
4.5
via the
cosection localization
technique of Kiem-Li
[
KL
]
, as in
[
KiPa2
]
.
We also prove Theorem
4.6
as a corollary.

Let
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
be the moduli space of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs on
X
with fixed Chern character
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}
be the standard symmetric obstruction theory of
\curly P
in (
52
).

We first observe that the semi-regularity maps provide
cosections
.

Proposition 4.12
.

There exists a natural map

\mathsf{SR}:\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\to H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}

satisfying the following properties:

(1)

The fibre of the map
\mathsf{SR}
over
[(F,s)]\in\curly P
is the composition
19
19

19

The first map is given by the canonical quasi-isomorphism
C^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\cong\bigoplus_{i}h^{i}(C^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})[-i]
for any complex of
vector spaces
(
[
GM
, Prop. III.2.4]
).

\mathsf{SR}|_{(F,s)}:R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[2]\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{sr}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee},

where
\mathsf{sr}
is the semi-regularity map (Definition
4.8
(1)) for the associated complex
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}:=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F]
.

(2)

The square of the map
\mathsf{SR}
is

\mathsf{SR}^{2}=\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}\otimes 1:H^{1}(X,T_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\to H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}.

Here, we identified
H^{3}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})=H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}
via Serre duality and the Calabi-Yau 4-form, as in Proposition
4.9
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
denote the universal complex.
Consider the map

R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[2]\xrightarrow{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times X/\curly P}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X})[3]\xrightarrow{\operatorname{tr}^{3}}H^{3}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P},

where the third trace map is defined as (see footnote
19
)

\operatorname{tr}^{3}:R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X})[3]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{tr}}}{{\to}}R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}[3]\to H^{3}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}.

The map
\mathsf{SR}
is then defined by composing the above with the isomorphism
H^{3}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\cong H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}
. The functoriality of the Atiyah classes ensures that the fibres of
\mathsf{SR}
are the semi-regularity maps. This proves (1).

Next, we claim that
the square

\mathsf{SR}^{2}:H^{1}(X,T_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SR}^{\vee}}\mathbb{E}[-1]\cong\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SR}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}

is given by contraction with the Chern character

\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}):=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{At}^{2}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}))\in H^{2}(X,\Omega_{X}^{2})\otimes\Gamma(\curly P,\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}).

Statement (2) then follows because Proposition
3.33
implies that the Chern character
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
is a horizontal section, i.e.,

\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})=\gamma\otimes 1.

In order to prove the claim, it suffices to take hypercohomology
\mathbb{H}^{0}(\curly P,-)
of the composition
\mathsf{SR}^{2}
and show that this equals contraction with
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
.
By
[
BuFl1
, Prop. 4.2]
, the following triangle commutes

\textstyle{H^{1}(\curly P\times X,T_{\curly P\times X/\curly P})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\curly P\times X}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3}(\curly P\times X,\Omega^{1}_{\curly P\times X/\curly P}),}

where the top map is induced by
\mathsf{SR}^{\vee}
and the right map by
\mathsf{SR}
. Using the decomposition (see footnote
19
)

H^{m}(\curly P\times X,T_{\curly P\times X/\curly P})=\bigoplus_{i+j=m}H^{i}(X,T_{X})\otimes H^{j}(\curly P,\mathcal{O}_{\curly P})

and similarly for
\Omega^{1}_{\curly P\times X/\curly P}
, we obtain the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{1}(X,T_{X})\otimes H^{0}(\curly P,\mathcal{O}_{\curly P})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{H}^{0}(\curly P,\mathsf{SR}^{\vee})}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\mathbb{I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\curly P\times X}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{H}^{0}(\curly P,\mathsf{SR})}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\otimes H^{0}(\curly P,\mathcal{O}_{\curly P})}

as desired.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

The map
\mathsf{SR}
in Proposition
4.12
is the collection of all cosections associated to
(3,1)
-forms in
[
KiPa2
, Ex. 9.2]
. More precisely, if we choose a
(3,1)
-form
\xi\in H^{1}(X,T_{X})\cong H^{1}(X,\Omega^{3}_{X})
, then the composition

\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SR}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\xrightarrow{\xi}\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}

is exactly the cosection
\sigma^{\xi}
associated to the
(3,1)
-form
\xi
considered in
[
KiPa2
]
.

We sometimes call the symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
in the obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}
the
virtual cotangent complex
. Following this perspective, we define the
reduced virtual cotangent complex

\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
as follows:

Definition 4.14
.

Choose a non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
with respect to the bilinear form
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
.

(1)

We denote by

\mathsf{SR}_{V}:\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SR}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\twoheadrightarrow V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}

the composition.
Then the square

\mathsf{SR}^{2}_{V}=\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}|_{V}\otimes 1:V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SR}_{V}^{\vee}}\mathbb{E}[-1]\cong\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}

is an isomorphism by Proposition
4.12
(2).

(2)

We define
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}:=\mathrm{cone}(\mathsf{SR}_{V}^{\vee}[1])
. Since the natural map
\mathsf{SR}_{V}^{\vee}[1]
has a left inverse, we have a splitting

(63)

\mathbb{E}\cong\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}\oplus\left(V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}[1]\right).

Moreover,
(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})^{\vee}[2]\to\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[2]\xrightarrow{\theta}\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
induces a symmetric form on
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
and (
63
) is a splitting of symmetric complexes.

(3)

We denote by

\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}_{V}^{\mathrm{red}})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}_{V}^{\mathrm{red}})

the quadratic cone stack induced by the above symmetric form.

The main result in this subsection is the following
cone reduction
property:

Proposition 4.15
.

Given a non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
with respect to
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
,
there exists a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P})_{\mathrm{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})}

for a unique dotted arrow and where the square is cartesian.
Here the closed embedding
(\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P})_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
is given by (
54
).
20
20

20

When we use
\mathrm{red}
as a subscript, it denotes the associated reduced substack.

Proof.

We will form a commutative diagram

(64)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\curly P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P})_{\mathrm{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{l}_{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}}
\textstyle{V|_{\curly P}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{\curly P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\curly P}}

and claim that all the squares are Cartesian. Here,
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}:=\mathfrak{C}(\mathsf{SR}_{V}^{\vee}[1])
is the linear function induced by
\mathsf{SR}_{V}^{\vee}:V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\to\mathbb{E}[-1]
and
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}
is the quadratic function induced by
\theta:\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\to(\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E})[-2]
.
Indeed, the lower square in (
64
) is cartesian since the quadratic cone stack
\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
is defined as the zero locus of the quadratic function
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}
. The upper right square in (
64
) is cartesian since
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
is defined as the cone of
\mathsf{SR}_{V}^{\vee}[1]:V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}[1]\to\mathbb{E}
, see the proof of Lemma
4.16
.
The middle upper square is cartesian since (
63
) is a direct sum decomposition of
symmetric complexes
. More precisely, we have

(65)

\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}=\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}}\circ p_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{V}|_{\curly P}\circ p_{2}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})=\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\times_{\curly P}V|_{\curly P}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\curly P}

where
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
are the projection maps and
\mathfrak{q}_{V}:V\times V\to\mathbb{C}
denotes the quadratic function induced by
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}|_{V}
. Then the formula (
65
) implies that the triangle

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\curly P}}

commutes. Hence, the middle upper square in (
64
) is cartesian, as claimed. Finally, by Kiem-Li’s cone reduction lemma
[
KL
]
in the form of Lemma
4.16
below, we can find the dotted arrow that fits into the commutative diagram (
64
).
∎

We rephrase Kiem-Li’s cone reduction lemma as follows. This lemma is required to complete the proof of Proposition
4.15
.

Lemma 4.16

(cf.
[
KL
, Prop. 4.3]
)
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
be an obstruction theory
for an arbitrary Deligne-Mumford stack
\mathscr{X}
.
Let
\Sigma:\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\to F
be a map for a vector bundle
F
.
Then there exists a commutative diagram

(66)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0_{F}}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}})_{\mathrm{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{l}_{\Sigma}}
\textstyle{F}

with a unique dotted arrow and where the square is cartesian.
Here the closed embedding
(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}})_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
is induced by the obstruction theory
\phi
,
the linear function
\mathfrak{l}_{\Sigma}:=\mathfrak{C}(\Sigma^{\vee}[1]):\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\to F
is induced by the map
\Sigma
, and
the perfect complex
\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma}
is the cone of the map
\Sigma^{\vee}[1]:F^{\vee}[1]\to\mathbb{E}
.

Proof.

Note that the statement is local on
\mathscr{X}
.
Thus we may assume that
\mathscr{X}
is a quasi-projective scheme and
F=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\oplus r}
is a trivial vector bundle.
By the resolution property of
\mathscr{X}
, we can find a chain complex of vector bundles that represents
\mathbb{E}

\mathbb{E}\cong[E^{-e}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\to E^{-2}\xrightarrow{d^{-1}}E^{-1}\xrightarrow{d^{0}}E^{0}]

and a morphism of chain complexes that represents
\Sigma

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{E_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Sigma}}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where we denoted
E_{i}:=(E^{-i})^{\vee}
.
Then we also have

\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma}\cong[E^{-e}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\to E^{-2}\oplus F^{\vee}\xrightarrow{(d^{-1},\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\vee})}E^{-1}\xrightarrow{d^{0}}E^{0}].

Firstly, we will show that the square in (
66
) is cartesian. Indeed, note that the cokernels

D:=\operatorname{coker}(d^{-1}:E^{-2}\to E^{-1}),\quad D_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}:=\operatorname{coker}((d^{-1},\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\vee}):E^{-2}\oplus F^{\vee}\to E^{-1})

fit into a right exact sequence of coherent sheaves

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.85417pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.85417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.85417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.85417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.41112pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.41112pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.02364pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 117.02364pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

By taking the associated abelian cones, we obtain a cartesian square

\textstyle{C(D_{\widetilde{\Sigma}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathscr{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0_{F}}
\textstyle{C(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Sigma}|_{C(D)}}
\textstyle{F.}

Since
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})=[C(D)/E_{0}]
and
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma})=[C(D_{\widetilde{\Sigma}})/E_{0}]
, we obtain the desired cartesian square via descent.

Secondly, we will find a dotted arrow that fits into the commutative diagram (
66
). The uniqueness is obvious.
Consider the induced (2-term) perfect obstruction theory

\phi^{\tau}:\mathbb{E}^{\tau}:=[E^{-1}\xrightarrow{d^{0}}E^{0}]\xrightarrow{t}\mathbb{E}\xrightarrow{\phi}\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}

and an induced cosection

that fits into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\vee}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{E}^{\tau})^{\vee}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\tau}}

Since
F
is a trivial vector bundle, we may write

\Sigma^{\tau}=(\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{r}):(\mathbb{E}^{\tau})^{\vee}[1]\to F\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\oplus r}.

Then
\sigma_{i}:(\mathbb{E}^{\tau})^{\vee}[1]\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}
are cosections for the perfect obstruction theory

. Hence by
[
KL
, Prop. 4.3]
, we have

(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}})_{\mathrm{red}}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}((\mathbb{E}^{\tau})_{\sigma_{i}})

as substacks of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}^{\tau})
, where
(\mathbb{E}^{\tau})_{\sigma_{i}}:=\mathrm{cone}(\sigma_{i}^{\vee}[1])
.
Here
\mathfrak{C}((\mathbb{E}^{\tau})_{\sigma_{i}})
are set-theoretically the kernel cone stacks in
[
KL
]
.
Hence we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigcap_{i}\mathfrak{C}((\mathbb{E}^{\tau})_{\sigma_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0_{F}}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}})_{\mathrm{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{C}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{l}_{\Sigma}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}^{\tau})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{l}_{\Sigma^{\tau}}}
\textstyle{F}

where the squares are cartesian by the result in the previous paragraph. Hence the dotted arrow in the above diagram gives us the desired dotted arrow in (
66
).
∎

Now Theorem
4.5
is a direct corollary of Proposition
4.15
.

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

We first construct a reduced virtual cycle with respect to a maximal non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
. Here maximal means that
\dim(V)=\mathrm{rank}(\mathsf{B}_{\gamma})=\rho_{\gamma}
. Thus the composition

(67)

V\hookrightarrow H^{1}(X,T_{X})\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma}

is an isomorphism.

As in the proof of Theorem
4.1
(see also Remark
4.3
), we can construct a square root Gysin pullback

\sqrt{0^{!}_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})}}:A_{*}\left(\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\right)\to A_{*}\left(\curly P\right)

for the symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
.
Here the orientation of
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
is determined by the orientations of
\mathbb{E}
and
H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma}
via the canonical isomorphisms

\det(\mathbb{E})\cong\det(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\otimes\det(V)\cong\det(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\otimes\det(H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma})

of line bundles, induced by the decomposition (
63
) and the isomorphism (
67
).

By the cone reduction property in Proposition
4.15
, we may consider the fundamental class of the intrinsic normal cone
\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}
as a cycle class in
\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})
,

[\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}]\in A_{0}\left(\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})\right).

Indeed, this can be defined as the pushforward of the fundamental class
[\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}]\in A_{*}((\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P})_{\mathrm{red}})=A_{*}(\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P})
under the closed embedding
(\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P})_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})
.

Analogous to the definition of the non-reduced virtual cycle in (
58
), we define the
reduced virtual cycle
as

(68)

\left[\curly P\right]^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}:=\sqrt{0^{!}_{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})}}\left[\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}\right]\in A_{\mathrm{rvd}}\left(\curly P\right).

Then the reduced virtual dimension is

\mathrm{rvd}=\mathrm{vd}+\frac{1}{2}\dim(V)=n-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\gamma}.

We now claim that the reduced virtual cycle
[\curly P]^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
in (
68
) is independent of the choice of a maximal non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
. Note that a choice of a maximal isotropic subspace
V
of
H^{1}(X,T_{X})
is equivalent to a choice of a section of the surjection

H^{1}(X,T_{X})\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma}.

Thus, if we fix one splitting

H^{1}(X,T_{X})=H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma}\oplus\ker(\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}),

then any other splitting corresponds to a map
H^{1}(X,T_{X})_{\gamma}\to\ker(\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}).
Therefore, any two splittings are connected via a family of splittings parametrized by
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. A deformation argument proves the claim.
21
21

21

Generally, we need to be careful when we apply the cone reduction in the relative setting. But in this case, the map
\curly P\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is flat, so we can detect the cone reduction property fibrewise.

∎

Remark 4.17
.

We view the cosection localization (Proposition
4.15
) in this paper as an
orthogonal
version to the one in
[
KiPa2
]
.
Indeed, there are two types of cosections
\Sigma:\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\to F
in DT4 theory:

(1)

isotropic
cosections, i.e.,
\Sigma^{2}=0
,

(2)

non-degenerate
cosections, i.e.,
\Sigma^{2}
is an isomorphism.

In
[
KiPa2
]
, cosection localization for the first case is studied, whereas we introduced the second case in this paper.

In the second case, if the orthogonal bundle
(F,\Sigma^{2})
is of even rank and has a maximal isotropic subbundle, then we can reduce to the first case. Then both approaches give the same reduced virtual cycle.

However, for surfaces on Calabi-Yau 4-folds, there are
many
examples (e.g. Example
1.5
) that have an
odd
number of cosections. Therefore we indeed need cosection localization as in the second case for counting surfaces.

Remark 4.18
.

In general, the cone reduction property in Proposition
4.15
does not imply the existence of a reduced obstruction theory, see
[
MPT
, Appendix A]
.
However, in our situation for
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
, we will see in the next subsection that there indeed exists a reduced obstruction theory.

We now prove Theorem
4.6
. The implication
(\Rightarrow)
can be shown using the infinitesimal lifting criterion as in
[
Blo
, Thm. 7.3]
. We provide an alternative proof, more in line with DT4 theory, using the cone reduction property (Proposition
4.15
).

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

(
\Rightarrow
) Let
[(F,s)]\in\curly P
be a
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair for which the associated perfect complex
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}:=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{s}F]
is semi-regular.

Choose a maximal non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
with respect to
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
. We first claim that the restriction of the obstruction map
\mathsf{ob}
in (
60
) to
V

\mathsf{ob}|_{V}:V\hookrightarrow H^{1}(X,T_{X})\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}

is an isomorphism. Indeed, by Corollary
4.10
, the above map
\mathsf{ob}|_{V}
is injective. On the other hand, the semi-regularity of
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
implies that the obstruction map
\mathsf{ob}=\mathsf{sr}^{\vee}:H^{1}(X,T_{X})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}
is surjective. Hence we have

\dim(V)=\rho_{\gamma}=\mathrm{rank}(\mathsf{B}_{\gamma})=\mathrm{rank}(\mathsf{ob}^{*}(\mathsf{Y}))=\mathrm{rank}(\mathsf{Y})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}.

Therefore the above map
\mathsf{ob}|_{V}
is bijective. This proves the claim.

By the derived Darboux theorem
[
BBJ
, Thm. 5.18(ii)]
,
we can find a diagram

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U(t)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}

where
U
is a smooth affine scheme,
E
is an orthogonal bundle over
U
,
t\in\Gamma(U,E)
is an isotropic section, and
U(t)
is the zero locus of
t
in
U
, with a Zariski open embedding
U(t)\hookrightarrow\curly P
whose image contains
[I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}]\in\curly P
. Also,
the standard symmetric obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}
in (
52
) can be written as

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{T_{U}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{dt}
\textstyle{E|_{U(t)}^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{dt}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\Omega_{U}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}|_{U(t)}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\Omega_{U}|_{U(t)}}

where
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{U(t)/U}
is the ideal sheaf of
U(t)\subseteq U
.
Moreover,
by choosing a minimal Darboux chart (in the sense of
[
BBJ
, Def. 2.13]
),
we can further assume that the fibre of the map
dt:T_{U}|_{U(t)}\to E|_{U(t)}^{\vee}
at
[I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}]\in U(t)
is zero.

Consider the map
\mathsf{SR}_{V}
in Definition
4.14
(1). Then we have a diagram

\textstyle{\Omega_{U}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}
\textstyle{[T_{U}|_{U(t)}\to E|_{U(t)}^{\vee}\to 0]}
\textstyle{V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{U(t)}[1]}

where the last term is the stupid truncation and the dotted arrow is the composition. Since
U(t)
is affine, the dotted arrow is zero and we obtain a morphism of cochain complexes

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{T_{U}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{dt}
\textstyle{E|_{U(t)}^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{dt}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}}
\textstyle{\Omega_{U}|_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{U(t)}[1]}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{U(t)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

for some map
\widetilde{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}
.
By Proposition
4.12
(1), and the fact that the fibre of
dt
at
[(F,s)]
is zero,
the fibre of the map
\widetilde{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}
over
[(F,s)]\in\curly P
can be identified with the composition

(69)

\widetilde{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}|_{(F,s)}:\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{sr}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow V^{\vee}.

Since
\mathsf{ob}|_{V}
is an isomorphism, Proposition
4.9
implies that the map (
69
) is an isomorphism. Therefore, by shrinking
U
further, we may assume that the map
\widetilde{\mathsf{SR}_{V}}
is an isomorphism.

Consider
U(t)
as the zero section
U(t)\subset E|_{U(t)}
.
By the cone reduction property in Proposition
4.15
,
we have

\left[\frac{(C_{U(t)/U})_{\mathrm{red}}}{T_{U}|_{U(t)}}\right]=\left(\mathfrak{C}_{U(t)}\right)_{\mathrm{red}}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})=\left[\frac{U(t)}{T_{U}|_{U(t)}}\right]

as substacks of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\subseteq[E|_{U(t)}/T_{U}|_{U(t)}]
since
\ker(\widetilde{\mathsf{SR}_{V}})=0
.
Therefore, we have

(C_{U(t)/U})_{\mathrm{red}}=U(t).

Equivalently, the ideal

\bigoplus_{n>0}\frac{\mathcal{I}^{n}}{\mathcal{I}^{n+1}}\subseteq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\frac{\mathcal{I}^{n}}{\mathcal{I}^{n+1}}

is nilpotent. Hence, we have
\frac{\mathcal{I}^{n}}{\mathcal{I}^{n+1}}=0

for some
n
, and Nakayama’s lemma implies

(1-i)\cdot\mathcal{I}^{n}=0

for some
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Since
U
is integral and
U(t)
is nonempty, we have
\mathcal{I}=0
and thus
t=0
. Therefore,
U(t)=U
is smooth of dimension

\dim(U)=\left(\dim(U)-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{rank}(E)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{rank}(E)=\mathrm{vd}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\gamma}=\mathrm{rvd}.

This completes the proof of (
\Rightarrow
) since
U(t)
is an open neighborhood of
[I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}]\in\curly P
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Assume that
\curly P
is smooth of dimension
\mathrm{rvd}
at
[I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}]\in\curly P
. Choose a maximal non-degenerate subspace
V
of
H^{1}(X,T_{X})
with respect to
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
. By Corollary
4.10
, the restriction of the obstruction map to
V

\mathsf{ob}|_{V}:V\hookrightarrow H^{1}(X,T_{X})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{ob}}\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}

is injective. On the other hand, since
\curly P
is smooth at
[I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}]\in\curly P
, we have

\dim(V)=\rho_{\gamma}=2\cdot(\mathrm{rvd}-\mathrm{vd})=2\cdot\left(\mathrm{ext}^{1}-(\mathrm{ext}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{ext}^{2})\right)=\mathrm{ext}^{2}

where
\mathrm{ext}^{1}:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}
and
\mathrm{ext}^{2}:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}
. Hence the above map
\mathsf{ob}|_{V}
is bijective. Then the obstruction map
\mathsf{ob}
is surjective, and its dual
\mathsf{sr}
is injective (Proposition
4.9
). Therefore
[I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}]\in\curly P
is semi-regular.
∎

4.5.
Algebraic twistor family

In this subsection, we construct a
reduced obstruction theory
via the
algebraic twistor family
introduced by the second-named author and Thomas in
[
KT1
]
.

We use the notation from the previous subsections. Let
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
be the moduli space of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs
(F,s)
on a Calabi-Yau 4-fold
X
with fixed cohomological Chern character
\operatorname{ch}(F)=v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
.
The main result in this subsection is the following:

Proposition 4.19
.

Given a non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
with respect to
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
, the composition

(70)

\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}:\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}\to\mathbb{E}\xrightarrow{\phi}\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}

is also a symmetric obstruction theory, i.e.,
h^{0}(\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})
is bijective and
h^{-1}(\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V})
is surjective. Here the first map in (
70
) is given by the decomposition (
63
) and the second map is the standard symmetric obstruction theory in (
52
).

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.19
. By the deformation theory of algebraic varieties, any subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
corresponds to a fibre diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{V}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{V}}

where
\mathcal{X}_{V}\to\mathcal{A}_{V}
is a smooth projective morphism over the spectrum of the local Artinian
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
A_{V}:=\mathcal{O}_{V}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}=\mathbb{C}\oplus V^{\vee}
, where
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathcal{O}_{V}
denotes the maximal ideal of functions vanishing at the origin. Here
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{V}}=V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}_{V}}
and the (restriction) of the (classical) Kodaira-Spencer map

\mathsf{KS}_{\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V}}|_{X}:\Omega^{1}_{X}\to V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}[1]

corresponds to the given inclusion
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
via adjunction.
Let

\widetilde{v}=v\otimes 1\in H^{*}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})\cong H^{*}_{DR}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}A_{V}

be the unique horizontal lift of
v\in H^{*}_{DR}(X)
(see subsection
3.5
for the definitions of the de Rham cohomology and horizontal sections). Form a fibre diagram

(71)

\textstyle{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{V}}

where
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})
is the relative moduli space of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs on the fibres of
\mathcal{X}_{V}\to\mathcal{A}_{V}
. The key observation is the following:

Proposition 4.20
.

If
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
is a non-degenerate subspace, then the canonical inclusion map

{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\hookrightarrow{\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})

is an isomorphism of schemes.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
KT1
, Lem. 2.1]
and
[
KT1
, Prop. 2.2]
. We first claim that the
Hodge locus
of the horizontal section

\gamma\otimes 1\in H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})\cong H^{4}_{DR}(X)\otimes A_{V}

is just the unique closed point in
\mathcal{A}_{V}
. More precisely, the scheme-theoretical zero locus of the induced section

\overline{\gamma\otimes 1}\in\frac{H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})}{F^{2}H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})}

is the closed point in
\mathcal{A}_{V}
. Indeed, choose a subspace
W\subseteq V
and let

\mathcal{A}_{W}:=\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C}\oplus W^{\vee})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{V}

be the corresponding subscheme. Assume that the section
\overline{\gamma\otimes 1}
vanishes on
\mathcal{A}_{W}
, i.e.,

\overline{\gamma\otimes 1}|_{\mathcal{A}_{W}}=0\in\frac{H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{W}/\mathcal{A}_{W})}{F^{2}H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{W}/\mathcal{A}_{W})}.

Equivalently, the unique horizontal lift of
\gamma
over
\mathcal{A}_{W}
is a Hodge class.

By
[
Blo
, Prop. 4.2]
(cf. Lemma
4.11
(1)), we deduce that the composition

\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}|_{W}:W\hookrightarrow H^{1}(X,T_{X})\xrightarrow{\iota_{-}(\gamma)}H^{3}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\cong H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}

is zero. Hence we have

W\subseteq V\cap\ker(\mathsf{B}_{\gamma})=0

by the non-degeneracy of
V
. This proves the claim.

We will now show that any morphism

(72)

t:T\to{\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})

from an affine scheme
T
factors through
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
. Indeed, let

I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{T}}\xrightarrow{s}F]

be the family of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs that corresponds to the map (
72
), where

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{V}}

is the induced fibre diagram. By Proposition
3.33
, we have

t^{*}(\gamma\otimes 1)=\widetilde{\operatorname{ch}_{2}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}\in F^{2}H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{T}/T)

where
\widetilde{\operatorname{ch}_{2}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}\in F^{2}H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{T}/T)
is the unique horizontal lift of the Chern character
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}):=\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{At}^{2}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}))\in H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{T}/T}^{2})
. Thus we have

t^{*}(\overline{\gamma\otimes 1})=0\in\frac{H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{T}/T)}{F^{2}H^{4}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}_{T}/T)}.

Hence the map
t:T\to\mathcal{A}_{V}
factors through the zero locus of
\overline{\gamma\otimes 1}
, which is the unique closed point in
\mathcal{A}_{V}
by the result in the previous paragraph. Now the fibre diagram (
71
) proves the desired property.

Finally, since the map
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\hookrightarrow{\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})
is a closed embedding, the result in the previous paragraph proves that the map is an isomorphism.
∎

By Proposition
4.20
, the reduced obstruction theory of
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
can be obtained as the obstruction theory of
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})
.

Proof of Proposition
4.19
.

By Proposition
4.20
, the fibre diagram (
71
) can be expressed as

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the two triangles commute. Hence we have a canonical direct sum decomposition of the cotangent complexes

(73)

\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)/\mathcal{A}_{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{A}_{V}}|_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the upper arrows and the lower arrows are the canonical distinguished triangles (cf.
[
Qu
, Lem. 2.10]
).
Let

\phi_{\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V}}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V}}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)/\mathcal{A}_{V}}

be the standard
relative
obstruction theory for
{\curly P}_{\widetilde{v}}^{(q)}(\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V})\to\mathcal{A}_{V}
where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V}}=\mathbb{E}
by Proposition
4.20
.
We will consider the induced
absolute
obstruction theory. Indeed, form a morphism of distinguished triangles

(74)

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{SR}_{V}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V}}}
\textstyle{V^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)/\mathcal{A}_{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{V}}|_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}[1]}

for some dotted arrow
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
, where the upper distinguished triangle is induced by (
63
), the lower distinguished triangle is the truncation of (
73
), and the right square commutes by Lemma
4.21
below.
By the long exact sequence associated to (
74
), we can deduce that the map
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}:\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}
is an obstruction theory, in the sense of
[
BF
]
.
Moreover since the lower distinguished triangle in (
74
) splits, the obstruction theory
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}:\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}
is uniquely determined by the diagram (
74
). Thus the map
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
in (
74
) is equal to the composition (
70
)
since the composition

\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{\mathcal{X}_{V}/\mathcal{A}_{V}}}\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)/\mathcal{A}_{V}}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}

is the original (non-reduced) obstruction theory for
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
.
∎

We need the following compatibility result between the Kodaira-Spencer maps to complete the proof of Proposition
4.19
. Here we consider a more general version than needed for Proposition
4.19
. This will be used later in the proof of deformation invariance in Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
be a smooth projective morphism of relative dimension
4
over a quasi-projective scheme
\mathcal{B}
. Let
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B})
be the relative moduli space of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs on the fibres of
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
. Let

\textstyle{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{\widetilde{v}}(\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

be a fibre diagram. Then we have a commutative diagram

(75)

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}:=R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})_{0}[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{SR}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{Rf_{*}(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}^{1}\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})|_{\curly P}[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\mathsf{KS}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\curly P/\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}_{\curly P/\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}}|_{\curly P}[1]}

where the relative obstruction theory
\phi_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}
and the relative cosection
\mathsf{SR}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}
are given by the two components

\displaystyle\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}):\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}[1]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\curly P/\mathcal{B}}[1]

\displaystyle\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\curly P}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}):\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}[1]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}[1]

of the Atiyah class
\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
of the universal complex
\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
.
For
\phi_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}
, we use the adjunction
R\pi_{*}(-)\dashv L\pi^{*}(-)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}[4]
.

Proof.

The proof is similar that that of Lemma
4.11
.
Consider the diagram

(76)

\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}|_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\curly P}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}}|_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\curly P/\mathcal{B}}|_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{KS}_{\curly P/\mathcal{B}}}

where the left two triangles commute with the dotted arrows, and the right two triangles commute with the solid arrows.
Since
\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
factors through
\operatorname{At}_{\curly P\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
, the composition of the two horizontal arrows in (
76
) vanishes
