\omega_{\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}.

Indeed, by
[
Del1
, Thm. 5.5]
, the pushforward
f_{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})
is a vector bundle that commutes with base change. Since the fibre
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is Calabi-Yau,
f_{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})
is a line bundle. Consider the canonical map of line bundles

f^{*}f_{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})\to\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}

given by adjunction. Since it is an isomorphism over
0\in\mathcal{B}
, it is an isomorphism over an open subscheme of
\mathcal{X}
containing the fibre
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Since
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
is projective, by shrinking
\mathcal{B}
, we may assume that the map
f^{*}f_{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})\to\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}
is an isomorphism. Moreover, by shrinking
\mathcal{B}
again, we may assume that
f_{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}
, and thus
\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}\cong f^{*}f_{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
. It proves the claim.

Next, the vanishing result in Proposition
5.5
resolves issue (3). Indeed, since
[\curly P^{(q)}_{v_{0}}(\mathcal{X}_{0})]^{\mathrm{red}}\neq 0
by assumption, we have
0\in\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
. Replace
\mathcal{B}
by
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
. Then we may assume that the function
b\in\mathcal{B}\mapsto\rho_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{b}}
is constant.

Issue (4) is resolved by simply replacing
\mathcal{B}
by a 2-fold étale cover
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}|_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
is orientable (Remark
5.6
).

We resolve issue (5) via an infinitesimal trick.
(Alternatively, we can also use a
2
-fold étale cover of
\mathcal{P}
as in the proof of Proposition
5.5
.)
Assume that
p:\curly P\to\mathcal{B}
is not surjective. Since the image is closed, there exists a 1-dimensional integral closed subscheme
C\subseteq\mathcal{B}
such that
0\in C
and
C\not\subseteq\operatorname{im}(p:\curly P\to\mathcal{B})
.
By replacing
\mathcal{B}
by the normalization
\widetilde{C}\to C
of
C
, we may assume that
\mathcal{B}
is a smooth quasi-projective curve, and the (set-theoretical) image
\operatorname{im}(p:\curly P\to\mathcal{B})
consists of finitely many points.
By shrinking
\mathcal{B}
further, we may assume that the image contains a single point
0\in\mathcal{B}
.
Then we have
(\curly P)_{\mathrm{red}}=(\curly P_{0})_{\mathrm{red}}
and hence
\mathbb{E}
is orientable by Lemma
5.15
below.

Finally, we explain how to avoid issue (6). We follow the situation in the previous paragraph so that
\mathcal{B}
is a smooth curve and the set-theoretical image of
p:\curly P\to\mathcal{B}
contains a single point
0\in\mathcal{B}
.
Form a fibre diagram

\textstyle{\curly P_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\curly P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\{0\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}.}

Since
\curly P_{0}\hookrightarrow\curly P
is a nilpotent thickening and the Chow groups are invariant under nilpotent thickenings, we may write

[\curly P]^{\mathrm{red}}=j_{*}(\alpha)

for some
\alpha\in A_{*}(\curly P_{0})
. By Theorem
5.1
and the self-intersection formula, we have

[\curly P_{0}]^{\mathrm{red}}=i_{0}^{!}[\widetilde{\curly P}]^{\mathrm{red}}=i_{0}^{!}j_{*}(\alpha)=e(N_{0/\mathcal{B}})\cap\alpha=0

since the normal bundle
N_{0/\mathcal{B}}
is a trivial line bundle. This contradicts the assumption
[\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X)]^{\mathrm{red}}\neq 0
, and hence we conclude that
p:\curly P\to\mathcal{B}
is surjective.
∎

We need the Lemma
5.15
to complete the proof of Theorem
5.13
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mathscr{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
be a nilpotent embedding of quasi-projective schemes. Let
E
be an orthogonal bundle on
\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
. If
E|_{\mathscr{X}}
is orientable, then
E
is orientable.

Proof.

Consider the canonical short exact sequence of algebraic groups

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{SO(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{O(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\det}
\textstyle{\mu_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

where
n
is the rank of
E
.
Note that the orthogonal bundle
E
defines a function
\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{E}BO(n)
to the classifying stack
BO(n):=[\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})/O(n)]
of
O(n)
. Consider the fibre diagram

\textstyle{U|_{\mathscr{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BSO(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\textstyle{BO(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\det}
\textstyle{B\mu_{2}.}

Since
E|_{\mathscr{X}}
is orientable, the principal
\mu_{2}
-bundle
U|_{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathscr{X}
is trivial. Hence
U|_{\mathscr{X}}=U_{1}\bigsqcup U_{2}
for some open subschemes such that the compositions
U_{1},U_{2}\hookrightarrow U|_{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathscr{X}
are isomorphism. Since
U
and
U|_{\mathscr{X}}
have the same Zariski topology, we can decompose
U=\widetilde{U_{1}}\bigsqcup\widetilde{U_{2}}
by disjoint open subschemes
\widetilde{U_{1}},\widetilde{U_{2}}
extending
U_{1},U_{2}
. Then
\widetilde{U_{1}},\widetilde{U_{2}}\to\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
are both finite étale bijective maps. Hence they are both isomorphisms. This means that the
\mu_{2}
-bundle
U\to\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
has a section. Hence
E
is orientable.
∎

The following example illustrates Theorem
5.13
in some non-semi-regular cases.

Example 5.16
.

Let
X=S_{1}\times S_{2}
be a product of two K3 surfaces. Let
\beta_{1}\in H^{1,1}(S_{1})
,
\beta_{2}\in H^{1,1}(S_{2})
be two irreducible effective curve classes with
\beta_{1}^{2}=2h-2
,
h\geq 2
and
\beta_{2}^{2}=0
.
Let
v:=\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{C_{1}\times C_{2}})
for any
C_{1}\in|\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(\beta_{1})|
and
C_{2}\in|\mathcal{O}_{S_{2}}(\beta_{2})|
.
Then we have an isomorphism

(106)

\phi:|\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(\beta_{1})|\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\curly P^{(1)}_{v}(X)\,.

Under this isomorphism we have

[\curly P^{(1)}_{v}(X)]^{\mathrm{red}}=h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(\beta_{1}))\cdot[\mathrm{pt}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}]

for some orientation.

We briefly illustrate the argument. The proof will appear in one of the sequels to this paper.
By the choice of
\beta_{2}
, there exists an elliptic fibration
p:S_{2}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that
\beta_{2}
is the class of a fibre. By Proposition
4.35
the reduced virtual dimension of
\curly P^{(1)}_{v}(X)
is one.
Let
\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}_{|\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(\beta_{1})|\times S_{1}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}}]
be the universal pair on
|\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(\beta_{1})|\times S_{1}
. Consider the fibre diagram

\textstyle{S_{1}\times S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}}
\textstyle{S_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\times\operatorname{id}\times p}
\textstyle{S_{1}}
\textstyle{S_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\times\Delta}
\textstyle{S_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}

where
\Delta:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the diagonal. Then the universal pair
j_{*}\textup{pr}^{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
on
|\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(\beta_{1})|\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times X
induces the morphism (
106
). Using
[
Par1
, Prop. 1.18]
, we can show that
\phi
is an isomorphism and the reduced obstruction theory of
\curly P^{(1)}_{v}(X)
reduces to a 2-term obstruction theory on
|\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(\beta_{1})|\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

By Theorem
4.6
, the points of
\curly P^{(1)}_{v}(X)
are
not
semi-regular. Since the reduced virtual cycle of
\curly P^{(1)}_{v}(X)
is non-zero, Theorem
5.13
applies to the class
\gamma=\beta_{1}\cup\beta_{2}
.

Remark 5.17
.

The original version of the variational Hodge conjecture by Grothendieck
[
Gro1
]
is stated for all
geometric points
, while Definition
5.11
only considers
\mathbb{C}
-points. In the situation of Theorem
5.13
, since we have shown that the map
p:\curly P^{(q)}_{\widetilde{\gamma},P_{v}}(\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{B}
is surjective on
\mathbb{C}
-points, it is also surjective for all geometric points. Hence we also deduce the original form of the variational Hodge conjecture.

Remark 5.18
.

In the situation of Theorem
5.13
, we further speculate that
[{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)]^{\mathrm{red}}\neq 0
implies
smoothness
of the Hodge locus of
\gamma
in the moduli space of Calabi-Yau
4
-folds near
X
.
23
23

23

For simplicity we consider Calabi-Yau
4
-folds with
h^{0,1}=h^{0,2}=h^{0,3}=0
. Then there exists a moduli space of Calabi-Yau
4
-folds as a smooth separated Deligne-Mumford stack with quasi-projective coarse moduli space by Viehweg
[
Vie
]
(see also Kollár
[
Kol2
, Cor. 8.23]
) and the Bogomolov-Tian-Todorov theorem
[
Bog
,
Tia
,
Tod
]
.

We note that not much is known about singularities of the Hodge locus.
At least when the scheme-theoretical Hodge locus is reduced near
X
and
[{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)]^{\mathrm{red}}\neq 0
, then Proposition
5.5
proves smoothness near
X
since the dimension of the tangent space is constant near
X
.
We hope to pursue this direction in a future work.

Appendix A
Reductions via
(-1)
-shifted 1-forms

In this section, we construct a reduced
(-2)
-shifted symplectic derived enhancement on
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
.
In particular, this construction shows that the reduced obstruction theory constructed from the algebraic twistor family in section
4.5
satisfies the isotropic condition.

In subsection
A.1
, we present general reduction procedures by cosections that come from
(-1)
-shifted
closed
/
exact

1
-forms.
In subsection
A.2
, we show that the cosections on
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
induced by the semi-regularity map can be enhanced to
(-1)
-shifted
closed

1
-forms.

We use the following conventions/notations in this section:

(1)

We use the language of
\infty
-categories
in the sense of Lurie
[
Lur1
,
Lur2
]
.

•

We write
\mathsf{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,-)
for the mapping space of an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
.

We consider the following
\infty
-categories:

•

Let
\mathsf{cdga}
be the
\infty
-category of commutative differential graded
\mathbb{C}
-algebras and let
\mathsf{cdga}^{\leq 0}\subseteq\mathsf{cdga}
be the full subcategory of non-positively graded objects.

•

Let
\mathsf{dSt}
be the
\infty
-category of
derived stacks
, i.e.,
\infty
-sheaves on
\mathsf{cdga}^{\leq 0}
with respect to the étale topology.

•

Let
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
(resp.
\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
) be the symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category of graded mixed complexes (resp. graded complexes), see
[
PTVV
]
.

We use the weight-shift notation
E((m)):=\oplus_{p}E(p+m)
for any
E=\oplus_{p}E(p)\in\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

We use the language of
derived Artin stacks
of Toën-Vezzosi
[
TV1
]
.

•

All derived Artin stacks are assumed to be
of finite type
, i.e., their classical truncation are (higher) Artin stacks of finite type.

•

Let
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M})
be the symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on a derived Artin stack
\mathbb{M}
.
Let
f^{*}:\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M}_{2})\to\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M}_{1})
be the pullback functor for a morphism
f:\mathbb{M}_{1}\to\mathbb{M}_{2}
of derived Artin stacks and let
f_{*}
be the right adjoint.

•

A derived Artin stack
\mathbb{M}
is
homotopically finitely presented
if the cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M})
is perfect (see
[
TV1
, Prop. 2.2.2.4]
).

•

A derived Artin stack
\mathbb{M}
is a
derived scheme
if the classical truncation
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{cl}}
is a scheme.

(3)

Let
\mathsf{DR}(-):\mathsf{dSt}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
be the
\infty
-functor of
derived de Rham complexes
of Pantev-Toën-Vaquié-Vezzosi
[
PTVV
]
, defined as follows:

•

Let
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
(resp.
\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
) be the
\infty
-category of graded mixed commutative differential graded
\mathbb{C}
-algebras (resp. graded commutative differential graded
\mathbb{C}
-algebras), see
[
CPTVV
]
.

•

Define
\mathsf{DR}
as the right Kan extension of the
\infty
-functor
\mathsf{DR}_{\mathrm{aff}}|_{\mathsf{cdga}^{\leq 0}}:\mathsf{cdga}^{\leq 0}\to\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
, where
\mathsf{DR}_{\mathrm{aff}}
is the left adjoint of the
\infty
-functor
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathsf{cdga}:E\mapsto E(0)
.

Abusing notation, we use the same symbol
\mathsf{DR}(-)
to denote the compositions
\mathsf{dSt}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DR}}\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
and
\mathsf{dSt}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DR}}\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

(4)

Let
\mathsf{NC}(-):\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
(resp.
\mathsf{CC}(-):\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
) be the
\infty
-functor of
weighted negative cyclic complexes
(resp.
weighted cyclic complexes
), defined as the right adjoint (resp. left adjoint) of the
\infty
-functor of trivial mixed structures,
\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
(resp.
\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
)
:E\mapsto(E,\epsilon=0)
.

•

Abusing notation, we use the same symbols
\mathsf{NC},\mathsf{CC}
to denote the compositions
\mathsf{NC}\circ\mathsf{DR},\mathsf{CC}\circ\mathsf{DR}
.

A.1.
Three reductions of derived schemes

In this subsection, we revisit Kiem-Li’s cone reduction
[
KL
]
via
derived algebraic geometry
.
We will see that there are three levels of reductions for derived schemes.

We consider the following hierarchy of structures

{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left\{\text{schemes with obstruction theories}\right\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}{\hbox{\kern-157.77803pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\line@@}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces==\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.73544pt\raise-13318.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(b)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces==\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.73544pt\raise-13318.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(b)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\right\}.}\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise-11.97221pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left\{{\begin{matrix}\text{schemes with closed embedding of}\\
\text{their intrinsic normal cone into an abelian cone stack}\end{matrix}}\right\}.}$}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{
{\begin{matrix}\text{schemes with closed embedding of}\\
\text{their intrinsic normal cone into an abelian cone stack}
\end{matrix}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces

More precisely, the above two arrows can be given as follows:

(a)

For any homotopically finitely presented derived scheme
\mathbb{X}
, there is an induced obstruction theory

\phi:\mathbb{E}:=\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}}|_{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}

on the classical truncation
\mathscr{X}:=\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{cl}}
by
[
STV
, Prop. 1.2]
.

(b)

For any scheme
\mathscr{X}
with an obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
, there is an induced closed embedding

\iota:\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})

of the intrinsic normal cone
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}
into the abelian cone stack
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
.

In derived algebraic geometry,
(-1)
-shifted
1
-forms
are the natural analogs of
cosections
. We have a similar hierarchy for them:

(a)

Let
\alpha:\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{X}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}}[-1]
be a
(-1)
-shifted
1
-form on a homotopically finitely presented derived scheme
\mathbb{X}
.
Then we have an induced
cosection

\sigma:=\alpha|_{\mathscr{X}}^{\vee}:\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}

for the induced obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}:=\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}}|_{\mathscr{X}}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
on the classical truncation
\mathscr{X}:=\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{cl}}
.

(b)

Let
\sigma:\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}
be a cosection for an obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
on a scheme
\mathscr{X}
. Then we have an induced
linear function

\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}:=\mathfrak{C}(\sigma^{\vee}[1]):\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathscr{X}}

on the associated abelian cone stack
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
.

We now state the main result in this subsection.
We refer to Appendix
B
for the generalizations of intrinsic normal cones, obstruction theories, and abelian cone stacks to (higher) Artin stacks.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a homotopically finitely presented derived Artin stack.
Let
\phi:\mathbb{E}:=\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}}|_{\mathscr{X}}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
be the induced obstruction theory on the classical truncation
\mathscr{X}:=\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{cl}}
and
let
\iota:\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
be the induced closed embedding.

(1)

(Cone reduction,
[
KL
]
)

For any
(-1)
-shifted
1
-form
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(\mathbb{X};-1)
, we have a commutative diagram of cone stacks

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}_{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}})_{\mathrm{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\mathrm{red}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathscr{X}}}

for a unique dotted arrow where
\sigma:=\alpha|_{\mathscr{X}}^{\vee}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}:=\mathrm{cone}(\sigma^{\vee}[1])
.

(2)

(Obstruction theory reduction)

For any
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-form
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}^{1,\mathrm{cl}}(\mathbb{X};-1)
, we have a commutative diagram of complexes

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\vee}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\mathrm{red}}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}}

for a unique dotted arrow where
\alpha:=\widetilde{\alpha}_{0}
is the underlying
(-1)
-shifted
1
-form,
\sigma:=\alpha|_{\mathscr{X}}^{\vee}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}:=\mathrm{cone}(\sigma^{\vee}[1])
.

(3)

(Derived reduction,
[
STV
]
)
For any
(-1)
-shifted exact
1
-form
\widetilde{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\mathcal{A}^{1,\mathrm{ex}}(\mathbb{X};-1)\simeq\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{X};-1)
(see Remark
A.2
below),
we have a homotopy commutative diagram of derived Artin stacks

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\widetilde{\alpha}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{X}}[-1]}

for some
\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{red}}
where the square is homotopy cartesian and the triangle induces isomorphisms
\mathscr{X}\cong(\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{red}})_{\mathrm{cl}}\cong\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{cl}}
.

In Theorem
A.1
, we use the following definitions of shifted
closed
/
exact
forms.

Remark A.2
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a homotopically finitely presented derived Artin stack.

(1)

The space of
d
-shifted
p
-forms
is defined as the mapping space

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{p}(\mathbb{X};d):=\,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}}(\mathbb{C}((-p)),\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{X})[d-p])

\displaystyle\cong\,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{X})}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{X}},\wedge^{p}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}}[d]).

(2)

The space of
d
-shifted closed
p
-forms
is defined as the mapping space

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{p,cl}(\mathbb{X};d):=\,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}}(\mathbb{C}((-p)),\mathsf{NC}(\mathbb{X})[d-p])

\displaystyle\cong\,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Map}_{\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}}(\mathbb{C}((-p)),\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{X})[d-p]).

(3)

The space of
d
-shifted exact
p
-forms
is defined as

\mathcal{A}^{p,\mathrm{ex}}(\mathbb{X};d):=\,\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}}(\mathbb{C}((-p+1)),\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})[d-p+1]).

Then we have canonical maps of spaces

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p,\mathrm{ex}}(\mathbb{X};d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p,cl}(\mathbb{X};d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p}(\mathbb{X};d).}

Before we prove Theorem
A.1
, we explain how the three reductions in Theorem
A.1
are related.

Remark A.3
.

In the situation of Theorem
A.1
, we have the following:

(1)

The obstruction theory reduction in Theorem
A.1
(2) is equivalent to the
scheme-theoretical cone reduction
, i.e., there exists a commutative diagram of cone stacks

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}_{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathscr{X}}}

for some dotted arrow.
Hence the obstruction theory reduction in Theorem
A.1
(2) clearly implies the cone reduction in Theorem
A.1
(1).

(2)

The derived reduction in Theorem
A.1
(3) implies the obstruction theory reduction in Theorem
A.1
(2). Indeed, the commutative diagram of derived Artin stacks induces a commutative diagram of cotangent complexes

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}[1]=\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{red}}/\mathbb{X}}|_{\mathscr{X}}[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{DR}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\alpha}})_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}}|_{\mathscr{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{red}}}|_{\mathscr{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}}

where
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{red}}/\mathbb{X}}|_{\mathscr{X}}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{X}/\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{X}}[-1]}|_{\mathscr{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}[2]
. By composing with the canonical map
\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}}
, we obtain the desired obstruction theory reduction.

Note that the cone reduction in Theorem
A.1
(1) was shown by Kiem-Li
[
KL
]
(see Lemma
4.16
) and the derived reduction in Theorem
A.1
(3) is trivial. (This approach was first introduced by Schürg-Toën-Vezzosi
[
STV
]
.)
Thus the essential part of Theorem
A.1
is the obstruction theory reduction in Theorem
A.1
(2). The following
derived Poincaré lemma
(which was essentially shown by Brav-Bussi-Joyce
[
BBJ
]
) shows that
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-forms are always exact and hence Theorem
A.1
(2) follows from Theorem
A.1
(3).

Proposition A.4
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a homotopically finitely presented derived Artin stack.
Then the canonical map

\mathcal{A}^{1,\mathrm{ex}}(\mathbb{X};-1)\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{A}^{1,\mathrm{cl}}(\mathbb{X};-1)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Since the statement is local, we may assume that
\mathbb{X}
is a derived affine scheme such that the associated analytic space of the classical truncation is connected.

Let
\mathsf{PC}(\mathbb{X})
be the cofibre of the map
B:\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})((-1))[1]\to\mathsf{NC}(\mathbb{X})
in
\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
induced by the map
\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{X})((-1))[1]\to\mathsf{NC}(\mathbb{X})
in
[
PTVV
, Rem. 1.6]
via adjunction.
Consider the induced morphism of cofibre sequences

(107)

\textstyle{\mathsf{NC}(\mathbb{X})(p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\mathsf{PC}(\mathbb{X})(p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})(p-1)[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{X})(p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})(p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})(p-1)[2]}

in
\mathsf{dg}
.
Note that we have canonical equivalences

(108)

\mathsf{PC}(\mathbb{X})(p)\cong\mathsf{PC}(\mathscr{X})(p)\cong R\Gamma(\widehat{\mathscr{Y}},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{\bullet})[2p]\cong R\Gamma(\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{an}},\mathbb{C})[2p]

in
\mathsf{dg}
where
\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{\bullet}
is Hartshorne’s algebraic de Rham complex
[
Har
]
for an embedding
\mathscr{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{Y}
into a smooth scheme
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{an}}
is the analytic space associated to
\mathscr{X}
.
Based on the HKR isomorphism
[
TV2
,
BZN
]
(see also
[
Hoy
]
),
the first equivalence follows from Goodwillie’s nil-invariance
[
Goo1
, Thm. IV.2.1]
,
the second equivalence follows from the Feigin-Tsygan theorem
[
FT
, Thm. 5]
(see also
[
Emm
, Thm. 2.2]
and
[
Wei3
, Thm. 3.4]
),
and the third equivalence follows from Hartshorne’s comparison theorem
[
Har
, Thm. IV.1.1]
.

We first claim that the canonical map given by the upper sequence in (
107
)

(109)

\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})(0)[-1])\xrightarrow{B}\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{NC}(\mathbb{X})(1)[-2])

is an equivalence. Indeed, since
H^{k}(-)
of (
108
) vanishes for
p=1
and
k\leq-3
, it suffices to show that

H^{-2}(\mathsf{PC}(\mathbb{X})(1))\xrightarrow{B}H^{0}(\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})(0))

is injective.
Then we have a commutative square

\textstyle{H^{-2}(\mathsf{PC}(\mathbb{X})(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathsf{CC}^{0}(\mathbb{X})(0))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{-2}(\mathsf{PC}(\mathbb{X}))(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathsf{CC}^{0}(\mathbb{X})(0))}

where the left vertical arrow is bijective by (
108
) and the lower horizontal arrow is injective by
[
Emm
, Prop. 2.6]
.
Hence we have the desired injectivity.

Then the canonical map given by the lower sequence in (
107
)

(110)

\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{X})(0)[-1])\xrightarrow{I}\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})(0)[-1])

is an equivalence since
\mathsf{CC}(\mathbb{X})(-1)\cong 0
.
By combining the two equivalences (
109
) and (
110
), we can deduce that the canonical map

\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{X})(0)[-1])\xrightarrow{d_{DR}}\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{NC}(\mathbb{X})(1)[-2])

is an equivalence as desired.
∎

Remark A.5
.

The derived Poincaré lemma also appears in
[
AKLPR
, Lem. 6.11]
.
We refer readers who are interested in a generalization of Kiem-Li’s cosection localization
[
KL
]
, using derived algebraic geometry and homotopical methods in intersection theory, to
[
AKLPR
]
(we only consider the cone reduction part in this paper).

A.2.
Shifted closed
1
-forms on moduli of perfect complexes

In this subsection, we prove that the cosections given by the semi-regularity maps can be enhanced to shifted
closed

1
-forms.
This is achieved by generalizing the
integration map
of
[
PTVV
]
and defining the
hat product
for derived mapping stacks.

We first generalize the
integration map
in
[
PTVV
]
for Calabi-Yau varieties to arbitrary smooth projective varieties.

Theorem A.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
n
.
Let
\mathbb{M}
be a derived Artin stack.
Then there exists a natural map

\int_{\pi}(-):\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})\to\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})((-n))

in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
where
\pi:X\times\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{M}
is the projection map.
Consequently, we have a natural homotopy-commutative square

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p,\mathrm{cl}}(X\times\mathbb{M},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\int_{\pi}(-)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p-n,\mathrm{cl}}(\mathbb{M},i-n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p}(X\times\mathbb{M},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\int_{\pi}(-)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p-n}(\mathbb{M},i-n).}

Proof.

By the Künneth formula in Lemma
A.7
below, we have a canonical equivalence

\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})\otimes\mathsf{DR}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})

in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.
Hence it suffices to construct a natural integration map for
X
,

\int_{X}(-):\mathsf{DR}(X)\to\mathbb{C}((-n))

in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
. Equivalently, it suffices to construct a natural map

\mathsf{CC}(X)(n)\to\mathbb{C}

in
\mathsf{dg}
since we have an adjunction
\mathsf{CC}(-)(n)\dashv(-)((-n))
.
The canonical equivalence

H^{0}(\mathsf{CC}(X)(n))\cong\mathbb{H}^{2n}(X,\Omega^{\leq n}_{X})\xleftarrow{\cong}H^{n}(X,\Omega^{n}_{X})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{C}

gives us the desired map.
∎

We provide a Künneth formula for
\mathsf{DR}
in the following lemma. This lemma is required to complete the proof of Theorem
A.6
.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. Let
\mathbb{M}
be a derived Artin stack.
Then there is a canonical equivalence

\mathsf{DR}(X)\otimes\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})\to\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})

in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

Proof.

Form a homotopy-cartesian square of derived Artin stacks

(111)

\textstyle{X\times\mathbb{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C}).}

Consider the canonical map

(112)

q^{*}(-)\cup\pi^{*}(-):\mathsf{DR}(X)\otimes\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})\to\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})

in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
given by the universal property of the coproduct.
It suffices to show that the map (
112
) induces an equivalence in
\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
since the forgetful functor

\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}

is conservative.

Note that we have a canonical equivalence

(113)

\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})\cong\bigoplus_{p}R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},\wedge^{p}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}}[p])

in
\mathsf{cdga}^{\mathrm{gr}}
, if we forget the mixed structure in
\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})
(see
[
CPTVV
, Rem. 2.4.4]
).
Moreover, since we have a canonical equivalence
\mathbb{L}_{X\times\mathbb{M}}\cong q^{*}(\mathbb{L}_{X})\oplus\pi^{*}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})
in
\mathsf{D}(X\times\mathbb{M})
, we also have an induced canonical equivalence

\wedge^{p}\mathbb{L}_{X\times\mathbb{M}}\cong\bigoplus_{p=p_{1}+p_{2}}q^{*}(\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes\pi^{*}(\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})

in
\mathsf{D}(X\times\mathbb{M})
.
Applying (
113
) for
X,\mathbb{M},X\times\mathbb{M}
to the canonical map (
112
), it suffices to show that the canonical map

(114)

R\Gamma(X,\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})\to R\Gamma(X\times\mathbb{M},q^{*}(\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes\pi^{*}(\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}}))

in
\mathsf{dg}
is an equivalence.

We will factor the map (
114
) as follows: Firstly, consider the canonical map

(115)

\pi_{*}q^{*}(\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}}\to\pi_{*}(q^{*}(\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes\pi^{*}(\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}}))

in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M})
given by the adjunction
\pi^{*}\dashv\pi_{*}
.
Secondly, consider the canonical base change map

(116)

R\Gamma(X,\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{M}}\to\pi_{*}q^{*}(\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})

in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M})
given by the cartesian square (
111
).
Finally, consider the canonical map

(117)

R\Gamma(X,\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{M})\to R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},R\Gamma(X,\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})

in
\mathsf{dg}
given by the adjunction
(-)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{M}}\dashv R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},-)
.
Then the map (
114
) is the composition of (
117
),
R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},(-)\otimes\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})
of the map (
116
), and
R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},-)
of the map (
115
).
Hence it suffices to show that the three maps (
115
),(
116
),(
117
) are equivalences.

The map (
115
) is the projection formula for
\pi:X\times\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{M}

and the map (
116
) is the base change formula for the square (
111
).
Both of these maps are equivalences by
[
BZFN
, Prop. 3.10]
since
X\to\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})
is a perfect morphism in the sense of
[
BZFN
, Def. 3.2]
by
[
BZFN
, Cor. 3.23, Prop. 3.19]
.

The map (
117
)
is an equivalence since
R\Gamma(X,\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})
is a perfect complex in
\mathsf{dg}
and both the functors
(-)\otimes R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}}),R\Gamma(\mathbb{M},(-)\otimes\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})
commute with finite colimits.
∎

We compare the integration map in Theorem
A.6
and the one in
[
PTVV
]
.

Remark A.8
.

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau
n
-fold with fixed Calabi-Yau
n
-form
\omega:\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Omega^{n}_{X}
.
Let
\mathbb{M}
be a derived Artin stack.
Then the integration map in
[
PTVV
]
can be expressed as

(118)

\int_{\pi,\mathrm{PTVV}}(-)\cong\int_{\pi}((-)\cup q^{*}\omega):\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})\to\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})[-n]

where
q:X\times\mathbb{M}\to X
and
\pi:X\times\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{M}
are the projection maps.
More precisely, the right hand side of the equivalence (
118
) is defined as the composition

\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}\otimes\omega}\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})\otimes\mathsf{DR}(X)((n))[-n]\\
\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}\otimes q^{*}}\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})\otimes\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})((n))[-n]\\
\xrightarrow{\cup}\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})((n))[-n]\xrightarrow{\int_{\pi}}\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})[-n]

where
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{n}_{X})\cong\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,\Omega^{\geq n}_{X})
is regarded as an element in

\pi_{0}\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dg}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{NC}(X)(n)[-n])\cong\pi_{0}\mathsf{Map}_{\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{DR}(X)((n))[-n])

and the cup product
\cup
is given by the multiplicative structure of
\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})
.

We now explain how we can obtain the equivalence (
118
). Recall that the integration map in
[
PTVV
]
is based on the construction of the canonical map

\kappa:\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})\to R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\otimes\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})

in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.
This can be reinterpreted as the composition

\mathsf{DR}(X\times\mathbb{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{DR}(X)\otimes\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})\to R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\otimes\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{M})

where the first arrow is given by the Künneth formula in Lemma
A.7
and the second arrow is induced by the canonical map
\mathsf{DR}(X)\to R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
that corresponds to the canonical isomorphism
\mathsf{CC}(X)(0)\xrightarrow{\cong}R\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\leq 0}_{X})
via adjunction.
Hence it suffices to show that

\textstyle{\int_{X,\mathrm{PTVV}}{(-)}\cong\int_{X}{(-)\cup\omega}:\mathsf{DR}(X)\to\mathbb{C}[-n]}

holds in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
. This follows from the homotopy-commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathsf{CC}(X)(0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{(-)\cup\omega}
\textstyle{R\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\leq 0}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)\cup\omega}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{R\Gamma(X,\Omega^{n}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{n}(X,\Omega^{n}_{X})[-n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}[-n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{CC}(X)(n)[-n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{R\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\leq n}_{X}[n])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\leq n}_{X}[n])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{2n}(X,\Omega^{\leq n}_{X})[-n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}[-n]}

in
\mathsf{dg}
via adjunction.

We note that the integration map in Theorem
A.6
without the closing structures can be described quite simply as follows.

Remark A.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
n
.
Let
\mathbb{M}
be a derived Artin stack. Then the integration map

\int_{\pi}(-):\mathcal{A}^{p}(X\times\mathbb{M};i)\to\mathcal{A}^{p-n}(X;i-n)

can be described as follows: Consider the projection map

\wedge^{p}\mathbb{L}_{X\times\mathbb{M}}\cong\bigoplus_{p=p_{1}+p_{2}}q^{*}(\wedge^{p_{1}}\mathbb{L}_{X})\otimes\pi^{*}(\wedge^{p_{2}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})\to\pi^{*}(\wedge^{p-n}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})\otimes q^{*}(\Omega^{n}_{X})

in
\mathsf{D}(X\times\mathbb{M})
. The adjunction
\pi_{*}\dashv\pi^{!}\cong\pi^{*}\otimes q^{*}(\Omega^{n}_{X})[n]
gives us a canonical map

\pi_{*}(\wedge^{p}\mathbb{L}_{X\times\mathbb{M}})\to\wedge^{p-n}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}}[-n]

in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M})
. Applying
\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{M})}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{M}}[-i],-)
, we obtain the above integration map.

Based on the generalized integration map in Theorem
A.6
, we can define the
hat products
for
derived mapping stacks
.
We first collect some basic facts on derived mapping stacks.

Remark A.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety.
Let
\mathbb{Y}
be a derived Artin stack.
The derived mapping stack
R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})
is the derived stack determined by the adjunction

\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dSt}}(\mathbb{U},R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y}))\cong\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{dSt}}(X\times\mathbb{U},\mathbb{Y})

for any
\mathbb{U}\in\mathsf{dSt}
.
By
[
HLP
]
,
R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})
is a derived Artin stack when
\mathbb{Y}
has a quasi-affine diagonal or the classical truncation of
\mathbb{Y}
is a
1
-Artin stack with affine stabilizers.

The cotangent complex can be computed as follows. For simplicity, assume that
\mathbb{Y}
is homotopically finitely presented and has quasi-affine diagonal. Let

\operatorname{ev}:X\times R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})\to\mathbb{Y}

be the evaluation map. By taking differential, we have a canonical map

\operatorname{ev}_{*}:\mathbb{T}_{X\times{R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})}}\cong q^{*}(T_{X})\oplus\pi^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})})\to\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{Y}})

where
\pi,q
are the projection maps.
The second component of
\operatorname{ev}_{*}
induces an isomorphism

(\operatorname{ev}_{*})_{2}:\mathbb{T}_{R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})}\cong\pi_{*}\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{Y}}).

in
\mathsf{D}({R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})})
by
[
HLP
, Prop. 5.1.10]
.

We note that the first component of
\operatorname{ev}_{*}
gives us

R\Gamma(X,T_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})}}\cong\pi_{*}q^{*}T_{X}\xrightarrow{(\operatorname{ev}_{*})_{1}}\pi_{*}\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{Y}})\xleftarrow[\cong]{(\operatorname{ev}_{*})_{2}}\mathbb{T}_{{R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})}},

and thus we obtain a canonical map

(119)

\widehat{-}:H^{i}(X,T_{X})\to\mathbb{H}^{i}({R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})},\mathbb{T}_{R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})})

between shifted vector fields.

We now define the
hat product
for a derived mapping stack.

Definition A.11
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
n
.
Let
\mathbb{Y}
be a derived Artin stack.
Assume that
R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})
is representable by a derived Artin stack.
We define the
hat product

\widehat{-\cdot-}:\mathsf{DR}(X)\otimes\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{Y})\to\mathsf{DR}(R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y}))((-n))

as the composition

\mathsf{DR}(X)\otimes\mathsf{DR}(\mathbb{Y})\xrightarrow{q^{*}(-)\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(-)}\mathsf{DR}(X\times R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y}))\xrightarrow{\int_{\pi}}\mathsf{DR}(R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y}))((-n))

in
\epsilon\textrm{-}\mathsf{dg}^{\mathrm{gr}}
, where
\operatorname{ev}
is the evaluation map and
\pi,q
are the projection maps.
Consequently, we also have a homotopy-commutative square

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p,\mathrm{cl}}(X;i)\times\mathcal{A}^{q,\mathrm{cl}}(\mathbb{Y};j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{-\cdot-}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p+q-n,\mathrm{cl}}(R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y});i+j-n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p}(X;i)\times\mathcal{A}^{q}(\mathbb{Y},j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{-\cdot-}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{p+q-n}(R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y});i+j-n).}

We can describe the shifted symplectic forms of
[
PTVV
]
on the derived moduli spaces of perfect complexes in terms of the hat product.

Remark A.12
.

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau
n
-fold with a fixed Calabi-Yau
n
-form
\omega:\mathcal{O}_{X}\cong\Omega^{n}_{X}
.

Let
R{\curly Perf}
be the derived moduli stack of perfect complexes (of fixed tor-amplitude
[a,b]
).
Then the cotangent complex can be computed as

\mathbb{L}_{R{\curly Perf}}\cong\mathbb{I}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{I}[-1]

for the universal complex
\mathbb{I}
on
R{\curly Perf}
.
Moreover, the
2
-shifted non-degenerate
2
-form on
R{\curly Perf}
given by the duality

(\mathbb{L}_{R{\curly Perf}}[1])^{\vee}\cong\mathbb{L}_{R{\curly Perf}}[1]

can be enhanced to a
2
-shifted symplectic
2
-form

\Theta\in\mathcal{A}^{2,\mathrm{cl}}(R{\curly Perf};2)

using the negative cyclic homology valued second Chern character of
\mathbb{I}

[
TV3
]
.

Let
R{\curly Perf}(X):=R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,R{\curly Perf})
be the derived moduli stack of perfect complexes on
X
.
Then

\theta:=\widehat{\omega\cdot\Theta}=\int_{\pi}q^{*}\omega\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}\Theta\in\mathcal{A}^{2,\mathrm{cl}}(R{\curly Perf}(X),2-n)

is the
(2-n)
-shifted symplectic form in
[
PTVV
]
.

We now show that the cosections associated to the semi-regularity maps can be enhanced to
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-forms.

Proposition A.13
.

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau
n
-fold with a fixed Calabi-Yau
n
-form
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{n}_{X})
.
For each
(n-3)
-shifted vector field
\xi\in H^{n-3}(X,T_{X})
, we can form a
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-form

\alpha_{\xi}\in\mathcal{A}^{1,\mathrm{cl}}(R{\curly Perf}(X);-1)

such that the induced cosection on the obstruction theory
(R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{I})[1])^{\vee}\to\mathbb{L}_{{\curly Perf}(X)}
of the classical truncation
{\curly Perf}(X):=R{\curly Perf}(X)_{\mathrm{cl}}
is equal to the composition

R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{I})[2]\xrightarrow{\operatorname{At}(\mathbb{I})}R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{X\times{\curly Perf}(X)})[3]\\
\xrightarrow{}R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{I}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X})[3]\xrightarrow{\operatorname{tr}}R\Gamma(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\curly Perf}(X)}[3]\\
\xrightarrow{}H^{3}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\curly Perf}(X)}\xrightarrow{-\int_{X}(-)\cup\iota_{\xi}\omega}\mathcal{O}_{{\curly Perf}(X)}

where
\mathbb{I}
is the universal complex.

Proof.

Note that we have

H^{2n-4}(X,\Omega^{\geq n-1}_{X})=\bigoplus_{p\geq n-1}H^{2n-4-p}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X})\subseteq H^{2n-4}_{DR}(X)

by the Hodge decomposition.
Hence
\iota_{\xi}\omega\in H^{n-3}(X,\Omega^{n-1}_{X})
induces an element

[\iota_{\xi}\omega]:=(\iota_{\xi}\omega,0,\ldots,0)\in H^{2n-4}(X,\Omega^{\geq n-1}_{X}).

We define the
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-form as the following hat product in Definition
A.11

\alpha_{\xi}:=\widehat{[\iota_{\xi}\omega]\cdot\Theta}\in\mathcal{A}^{1,\mathrm{cl}}(R{\curly Perf}(X;-1)).

It remains to compute the induced cosection of
\alpha_{\xi}
in the classical truncation. By Lemma
A.14
below, the underlying
(-1)
-shifted
1
-form can be written as

(\alpha_{\xi})_{0}=\widehat{(\iota_{\xi}\omega)\cdot\Theta_{0}}=\iota_{\widehat{\xi}}(\widehat{\omega\cdot\Theta_{0}})=\iota_{\widehat{\xi}}(\theta_{0})\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(R{\curly Perf}(X);-1)

where
\widehat{\xi}
is defined by (
119
) in Remark
A.10
.
Here
(-)_{0}
denotes the underlying form after forgetting the closing structure as in Remark
A.2
.
By
[
STV
, Rem. A.1]
, we have

\operatorname{ev}_{*}=\operatorname{At}(\mathbb{I}):\mathbb{T}_{X\times R{\curly Perf}(X)}\to R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{I})[1]

for the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}:X\times R{\curly Perf}(X)\to R{\curly Perf}
.
Since
\widehat{\xi}
is defined by the first composition of
\operatorname{ev}_{*}
, we have the desired formula by taking dual and classical truncation.
∎

We need the following technical lemma to complete the proof of Proposition
A.13
.
This formula is inspired by
[
Viz
, Lem. 8]
.

Lemma A.14
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
n
.
Let
\mathbb{Y}
be a homotopically finitely presented derived Artin stack.
Assume that
R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})
is a derived Artin stack.
Then we have

\iota_{\widehat{\xi}}(\widehat{\alpha\cdot\beta})\cong-\widehat{(\iota_{\xi}\alpha)\cdot\beta}\in\mathcal{A}^{p+q-n-1}(R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y});i+j-n+k)

for
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{p}(X;i)
,
\beta\in\mathcal{A}^{q}(\mathbb{Y};j)
,
\xi\in H^{k}(X,T_{X})
, where
\widehat{\xi}
is defined by (
119
) in Remark
A.10
.

Proof.

For simplicity of notation,
let
\mathbb{M}:=R\underline{\mathsf{Map}}(X,\mathbb{Y})
and

\widetilde{\xi}:=(q^{*}\xi,-\pi^{*}\widehat{\xi})\in\mathbb{H}^{k}(X\times\mathbb{M},\mathbb{T}_{X\times\mathbb{M}})=H^{k}(X\times\mathbb{M},q^{*}\mathbb{T}_{X})\oplus\mathbb{H}^{k}(X\times\mathbb{M},\pi^{*}\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{M}}).

By the definition of
\widehat{\xi}
in (
119
), we have

\operatorname{ev}_{*}(\widetilde{\xi})=0\in\mathbb{H}^{k}(X\times\mathbb{M},\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{Y}})).

Firstly, we have

\displaystyle\iota_{\widetilde{\xi}}(q^{*}(\alpha)\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta))

\displaystyle=(\iota_{q_{*}\widetilde{\xi}}q^{*}(\alpha))\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta)\pm q^{*}(\alpha)\cup(\iota_{\operatorname{ev}_{*}\widetilde{\xi}}\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta))

\displaystyle=q^{*}(\iota_{\xi}(\alpha))\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta)

in
\mathbb{H}^{i+j+k}(X\times\mathbb{M},\wedge^{p+q-1}\mathbb{L}_{X\times\mathbb{M}})
.

Secondly, we have

\int_{\pi}\iota_{\widetilde{\xi}}(q^{*}(\alpha)\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta))=-\iota_{\widehat{\xi}}\int_{\pi}(q^{*}(\alpha)\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta))

in
\mathbb{H}^{i+j+k-n}(\mathbb{M},\wedge^{p+q-n-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{M}})
(see Remark
A.9
).

Combining these two formulas, we have

\widehat{(\iota_{\xi}\alpha)\cdot\beta}=\int_{\pi}\iota_{\widetilde{\xi}}(q^{*}(\alpha)\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta))=-\iota_{\widehat{\xi}}\int_{\pi}(q^{*}(\alpha)\cup\operatorname{ev}^{*}(\beta))=-\iota_{\widehat{\xi}}(\widehat{\alpha\cdot\beta}),

which completes the proof.
∎

Now we have the desired reduced obstruction theory as follows:

Corollary A.15
.

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau
4
-fold,
v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, and
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
.
Let
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
and let
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}
be the standard symmetric obstruction theory.
Choose a non-degenerate subspace
V\subseteq H^{1}(X,T_{X})
with respect to the symmetric bilinear form
\mathsf{B}_{\gamma}
for
\gamma:=v_{2}\in H^{2}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})
.
Then we have a reduced obstruction theory
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}
that fits into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{SR}_{V}^{\vee}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{V}^{\mathrm{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\mathrm{red}}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}.}

Moreover, this reduced obstruction theory
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}
comes from a canonical
(-2)
-shifted symplectic derived enhancement.

Proof.

Choose a basis
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{\rho_{\gamma}}\in V
.
For the first statement, by Theorem
A.1
(2), it suffices to show that the associated cosections
\mathsf{SR}(\xi_{i}):\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[1]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{SR}}H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}\xrightarrow{\xi}\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}
can be enhanced to
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-forms on the standard derived enhancement
R\curly P
of
\curly P
.
By Proposition
A.13
,
\mathsf{SR}(\xi_{i})
are the underlying cosections of the
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-forms
u^{*}(\alpha_{\xi_{i}})
, where
u:R\curly P\to R{\curly Perf}(X)
is given by the universal complex on
X\times R\curly P
.

For the second statement, since the classical truncation
\curly P
is quasi-projective, by Proposition
A.4
, the closed
1
-forms
u^{*}(\alpha_{\xi_{i}})
can be lifted to unique exact
1
-forms
\widetilde{u^{*}(\alpha_{\xi_{i}})}\in\mathcal{A}^{1,\mathrm{ex}}(R\curly P;-1)=\mathcal{A}^{0}(R\curly P;-1)
.
By taking the derived zero locus of these
(-1)
-shifted functions, we have a reduced derived scheme
R\curly P^{\mathrm{red}}\subseteq R\curly P
which induces the reduced obstruction theory as above. Moreover, we can pullback the
(-2)
-shifted symplectic form
\theta\in\mathcal{A}^{2,\mathrm{cl}}(R\curly P;-2)
via the inclusion map
R\curly P^{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow R\curly P
, which is also non-degenerate.
∎

Remark A.16
.

In the situation of Corollary
A.15
, the existence of the reduced obstruction theory is equivalent to the scheme-theoretical cone reduction (see Remark
A.3
(1)).
Hence we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E}_{V}^{\mathrm{red}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}_{V}^{\mathrm{red}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{\curly P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}}
\textstyle{\curly P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\curly P}.}

In particular, the existence of a dotted arrow implies the isotropic condition.

The main difference between Corollary
A.15
and Proposition
4.19
is that the reduced obstruction theory in Corollary
A.15
is given as a
factorization
, whereas the one in Proposition
4.19
is given as a
composition
.
However note that by (
78
), the two actually coincide.

Remark A.17
.

The result in this section does not replace section
4
. Corollary
A.15
only replaces Kiem-Li’s cone reduction lemma in Lemma
4.16
. We still need the other arguments in section
4
to define an Oh-Thomas virtual cycle from Corollary
A.15
. Indeed, we need to compute the square of
\mathsf{SR}
to show that
\mathsf{SR}_{V}
is a
non-degenerate
cosection (see Remark
4.17
) and hence the induced symmetric form on
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{red}}_{V}
is non-degenerate.

Remark A.18
.

Corollary
A.15
provides a simple alternative proof of the Bloch conjecture for surface classes on Calabi-Yau
4
-folds, which was shown in general by Pridham
[
Pri
]
and Bandiera-Lepri-Manetti
[
BLM
]
(and by Iacono-Manetti
[
IM
]
when the normal bundle is extendable).

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau
4
-fold and
S\subseteq X
be a local complete intersection surface.
Consider an extension
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{A}}
of local Artinian
\mathbb{C}
-schemes such that
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\mathcal{J}=0
where
\mathcal{J}:=\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}/\overline{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq X\times\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathcal{A}
-flat extension of
S
.
Let

\mathsf{ob}(\mathcal{S})\in H^{1}(X,N_{S/X})\otimes\mathcal{J}\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(\mathcal{I}_{S/X},\mathcal{I}_{S/X})_{0}\otimes\mathcal{J}

be the obstruction of deforming
\mathcal{S}\subseteq X\times\mathcal{A}
to
X\times\overline{\mathcal{A}}
in
[
Blo
, Prop. 2.6]
.
By
[
Blo
]
, the obstruction
\mathsf{ob}(\mathcal{S})
lies in the kernel of the semi-regularity map

\mathsf{sr}:H^{1}(X,N_{S/X})\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(\mathcal{I}_{S/X},\mathcal{I}_{S/X})_{0}\to H^{3}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})

when the canonical map

(120)

d_{DR}:\mathcal{J}\to\Omega^{1}_{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}}

is injective. The Bloch conjecture states that the same result holds without the assumption on the injectivity of (
120
).
(See Proposition
4.24
for the comparison of the obstruction/semi-regularity maps for closed subschemes
[
Blo
]
and ideal sheaves
[
BuFl1
]
.)

Let
\curly P:=\curly P^{(-1)}_{v}(X)
be the Hilbert scheme of subschemes of Chern character
v=\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{S})
.
Let
u:\mathcal{A}\to\curly P:=\curly P^{(-1)}_{v}(X)
be the canonical map given by
\mathcal{S}
. By
[
BF
, Thm. 4.5]
, the obstruction
\mathsf{ob}(\mathcal{S})
is equivalent to the composition

u^{*}\mathbb{E}\xrightarrow{u^{*}\phi}u^{*}(\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)})\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{A}/\overline{\mathcal{A}}}\cong\mathcal{J}[1]

where
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)}
is the standard obstruction theory.
Instead of choosing a non-degenerate subspace
V
in Corollary
A.15
, we now consider all the cosections that come from
H^{1}(X,T_{X})
.
By the arguments in Corollary
A.15
, we can form a reduced obstruction theory
\phi^{\prime}
that fits into the diagram

\textstyle{H^{1}(X,T_{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\curly P}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{SR}^{\vee}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\curly P}.}

Hence the obstruction
\mathsf{ob}(\mathcal{S})
comes from the left term in the exact sequence,

\textstyle{h^{1}(\mathbb{E}^{\prime}|_{[S]}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{J})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{1}(\mathbb{E}|_{[S]}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{J})\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}(\mathcal{I}_{S/X},\mathcal{I}_{S/X})_{0}\otimes\mathcal{J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{sr}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(X,T_{X})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{J},}

which implies
\mathsf{sr}(\mathsf{ob}(\mathcal{S}))=0
.

Remark A.19
.

The results in this section can also be applied to the reduced curve counting theory for Calabi-Yau
3
-folds with holomorphic
2
-forms in
[
KT1
]
.
Indeed, Proposition
A.13
implies that the cosections associated to the semi-regularity map and a
(2,0)
-form in
[
KT1
]
can be enhanced to a
(-1)
-shifted closed
1
-form. This in particular provides reduced derived enhancements.

However, the reduced curve counting theory for
K3
surfaces in
[
MPT
]
does not require the result in this subsection
A.2
(but only the previous subsection
A.1
), and was already studied in
[
STV
]
.
The main reason is that the cosections in
[
MPT
]
do not involve the Atiyah classes and we can see directly that it comes from the de Rham differential of
(-1)
-shifted functions.

Appendix B
Moduli stacks of semi-stable sheaves

In this section, we generalize the results in the previous sections to the moduli
stack
of semi-stable sheaves.
In particular, we construct reduced Oh-Thomas virtual cycles on these moduli stacks and prove that they are deformation invariant along Hodge loci.

We will use the following notations/conventions in this section.

(1)

We use the language of
\infty
-categories
[
Lur1
,
Lur2
]
and derived algebraic geometry
[
TV1
]
as in Appendix
A
.

•

An
algebraic stack
will always be a classical
1
-Artin stack.

(2)

We use the following concepts developed by Aranha-Pstragowski
[
AP
]
.
These are natural generalizations of analogous notions for DM stacks in
[
BF
,
Man
]
.

•

To any algebraic stack
\mathscr{X}
and a derived category object
\mathbb{E}\in\mathsf{D}(\mathscr{X})
,
we can associate a
(higher) abelian cone stack

[
AP
, Section 3]

\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}):=\operatorname{Spec}\,\operatorname{Sym}\mathbb{E}[-1]:T\in\mathsf{Sch}\mapsto\mathsf{Map}_{\mathsf{D}(T)}(\mathbb{E}[-1]|_{T},\mathcal{O}_{T}).

When
\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{E}\cong[D\to B^{\vee}\to S^{\vee}]
for a coherent sheaf
D
and vector bundles
B,S
, then we write
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E}):=\left[\nicefrac{{C(D)}}{{\left[\nicefrac{{B}}{{S}}\right]}}\right]
.

•

Let
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}})
denote the
intrinsic normal cone
for a morphism of algebraic stacks
f:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
, see
[
AP
, Thm. 6.2, Thm. 6.3]
.

•

An
obstruction theory
24
24

24

In
[
AP
, Def. 8.1]
, the full cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}
is used in the definition of an obstruction theory, but here we use the truncated cotangent complex
\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}
.
is a morphism
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}
in
\mathsf{D}(\mathscr{X})
such that
h^{0}(\phi)
,
h^{1}(\phi)
are bijective, and
h^{-1}(\phi)
is surjective.
An obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}
induces a closed embedding
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
by
[
AP
, Prop. 8.2]
.

(3)

We consider the following generalizations of
[
OT1
,
Par1
]
.

•

We consider the
symmetric complexes
of tor-amplitude
[-3,1]
as in
[
Par1
, Def. C.1]
. They are perfect complexes
\mathbb{E}
on algebraic stacks
\mathscr{X}
equipped with
(-2)
-shifted symmetric forms
\theta:\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathbb{E}[-1])
such that
\iota(\theta):\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[2]\cong\mathbb{E}
.

Note that there is a
canonical quadratic function

\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathscr{X}}
associated to the symmetric form
\theta
.
We denote by
\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
the zero locus of
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}
.

•

An
orientation
of a symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
of tor-amplitude
[-3,1]
is an isomorphism of line bundles
o:\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}\to\det(\mathbb{E})
such that
\det(\iota(\theta))=(-1)^{\frac{r(r-1)}{2}}o\circ o^{\vee}
where
r=\mathrm{rank}(\mathbb{E})
.

•

A
symmetric obstruction theory
for a morphism
f:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
of algebraic stacks is a pair of a symmetric complex
\mathbb{E}
of tor-amplitude
[-3,1]
on
\mathscr{X}
and an obstruction theory
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}
for
f
.

B.1.
Reduced virtual cycles for Artin stacks

In this subsection, we extend Oh-Thomas virtual cycles
[
OT1
]
(more generally, square root virtual pullbacks
[
Par1
]
) and Kiem-Li’s cone reduction lemma
[
KL
]
to Artin stacks (which are global quotients).
Consequently, we have reduced Oh-Thomas virtual cycles for these Artin stacks.

We first generalize
Oh-Thomas virtual cycles

[
OT1
]
(and
square root virtual pullbacks

[
Par1
]
) to Artin stacks.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
f:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
be a morphism of algebraic stacks.
Let
\phi:\mathbb{E}\to\tau^{\geq-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}
be a symmetric obstruction theory of tor-amplitude
[-3,1]
such that

(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}})_{\mathrm{red}}\subseteq\mathfrak{Q}(\mathbb{E})
as substacks of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})
.
Let
o:\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}\to\det(\mathbb{E})
be an orientation.
Assume that
\mathscr{X}
is a global quotient stack, i.e., there exists a quasi-projective scheme
P
with a linear action of a linear algebraic group
G
such that
\mathscr{X}\cong[P/G]
.
Then there is a canonical square root virtual pullback

\sqrt{f^{!}}:A_{*}(\mathscr{Y})\to A_{*}(\mathscr{X}).

Moreover, for any cartesian square

\textstyle{\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathscr{Y}}

where
i
is a regular closed embedding, we have

(121)

i^{!}\circ\sqrt{f^{!}}=\sqrt{(f^{\prime})^{!}}\circ i^{!}:A_{*}(\mathscr{Y})\to A_{*}(\mathscr{X}^{\prime}).

Remark B.2
.

In the situation of Theorem
B.1
, when
\mathscr{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}\,(\mathbb{C})
, we define the
Oh-Thomas
virtual cycle as

[\mathscr{X}]^{\mathrm{vir}}:=\sqrt{f^{!}}[\mathscr{Y}]\in A_{*}(\mathscr{X}).

Then the formula (
121
) gives us the deformation invariance.

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.

We prove Theorem
B.1
in four steps.

Step 1.

We first show that there exists a
symmetric resolution

(122)

\mathbb{E}\cong[S\xrightarrow{e}B\xrightarrow{d}E\cong E^{\vee}\xrightarrow{d^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\xrightarrow{e^{\vee}}S^{\vee}]

as in
[
OT1
, Prop. 4.1]
.
More precisely, (
122
) is an equivalence in
\mathsf{D}(\mathscr{X})
for some vector bundles
B
,
S
and a special orthogonal bundle
(E,\mathfrak{q}_{E})
such that the symmetric form of
\mathbb{E}
is represented by

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota(\theta)}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{\vee}\circ\mathfrak{q}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{E}}
\textstyle{B^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{\vee}}
\textstyle{S^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{E}\circ d}
\textstyle{E^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{\vee}}
\textstyle{B^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{\vee}}
\textstyle{S^{\vee}.}

Since the global quotient stack
\mathscr{X}
has the resolution property
[
Tho
, Lem. 2.6]
, we can find a resolution
A^{\bullet}:=[A^{-3}\xrightarrow{a^{-2}}A^{-2}\xrightarrow{a^{-1}}A^{-1}\xrightarrow{a^{0}}A^{0}\xrightarrow{a^{1}}A^{1}]\cong\mathbb{E}
such that
\iota(\theta):\mathbb{E}^{\vee}[2]\to\mathbb{E}
is represented by a chain map
\theta^{\bullet}:(A^{\bullet})^{\vee}[2]\to A^{\bullet}
satisfying
(\theta^{\bullet})^{\vee}[2]=\theta^{\bullet}.
Let
S:=A^{-3}
and

B:=\operatorname{coker}((A^{1})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{((a^{1})^{\vee},\theta^{-3})}(A^{0})^{\vee}\oplus A^{-3}).

Then
B^{\vee}=\ker(A^{0}\oplus(A^{-3})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{(a^{1},\theta^{1})}A^{1})
and we have a quasi-isomorphism

c^{\bullet}:B^{\bullet}:=[A^{-3}\xrightarrow{a^{-2}}A^{-2}\xrightarrow{a^{-1}}A^{-1}\xrightarrow{b^{0}}B^{\vee}\xrightarrow{b^{1}}S^{\vee}]\xrightarrow{\cong}A^{\bullet}

where
b^{0}:=(a^{0},0)
,
b^{1}:=\mathrm{pr}_{2}
,
c^{\leq-1}:=\operatorname{id}
,
c^{0}:=\mathrm{pr}_{1}
, and
c^{1}:=\theta^{1}
.
Also
\theta^{\bullet}
factors through a self-dual map
\tau^{\bullet}:(B^{\bullet})^{\vee}[2]\to B^{\bullet}
. Since
\tau^{1}
is an isomorphism, we can apply
[
OT1
, Prop. 4.1]
to the middle three terms of
B^{\bullet}
. Let

E:=\operatorname{coker}(B\to(A^{-1})^{\vee}\oplus A^{-2})\cong\ker(A^{-1}\oplus(A^{-2})^{\vee}\to B^{\vee})=E^{\vee},

then we have (
122
) as claimed.
25
25

25

We thank Arkadij Bojko for pointing out an omission in an earlier version of Step 1.

Step 2.
We then construct
\sqrt{f^{!}}
as follows:
consider the fibre diagram

\textstyle{C(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left[\nicefrac{{E}}{{\left[\nicefrac{{B}}{{S}}\right]}}\right]}

where
D:=\operatorname{coker}(d:B\to E)
. The horizontal maps are closed embeddings and the vertical maps are
[\nicefrac{{B}}{{S}}]
-torsors. Then we have

\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{E}}|_{C(D)}=\mathfrak{q}_{E}|_{C(D)}

since the symmetric forms of
\mathbb{E}
and
E
map to the same (degenerate) symmetric form on
[S\xrightarrow{e}B\xrightarrow{d}E\to 0\to 0]
.
Let
\tau\in\Gamma(C(D),E|_{C(D)})
be the tautological section.
Then we have a localized Edidin-Graham class

(123)

\sqrt{e}(E|_{C(D)},\tau):A_{*}(C(D))\to A_{*}(\mathscr{X})

since
C(D)
is a global quotient stack.
See
[
KiPa2
, Rem. 3.3]
for the extension of the localized Edidin-Graham class
[

, Def. 3.2]
to the equivariant Chow groups
[
EG2
,
Tot
]
. Here the orientation of
E
is induced by the orientation of
\mathbb{E}
via the isomorphism
\det(E)\cong\det(\mathbb{E})
induced by the symmetric resolution (
122
).

Consider a global factorization

(124)

\textstyle{\mathscr{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathscr{Y}}

into a representable morphism
g
and a smooth morphism
h
for some algebraic stack
\mathscr{Z}
.
Such factorization exists since
\mathscr{X}
can be embedded into a smooth algebraic stack
\mathscr{W}
by assumption and we may take
g:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Z}:=\mathscr{W}\times\mathscr{Y}
. Let

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{C(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{E})}

be a fibre diagram of higher Artin stacks. Then we can form maps

(125)

A_{*}(\mathscr{Y})\xrightarrow{h^{*}}A_{*}(\mathscr{Z})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{sp}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Z}}}A_{*}(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Z}})\xrightarrow{r^{*}}A_{*}(\mathfrak{C})\xrightarrow{(s^{*})^{-1}}A_{*}(C)\xrightarrow{j_{*}}A_{*}(C(D))

between the Chow groups of algebraic stacks(=
1
-Artin stacks).
Here the specialization map
\mathrm{sp}_{\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Z}}
is well-defined as in
[
Man
]
since

is representable, and the smooth pullbacks

,

are isomorphisms by
[
Kre1
, Prop. 4.3.2]
since

is a
[B/S]
-torsor and

is a
\mathrm{Tot}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathscr{Z}/\mathscr{Y}})
-torsor.
By composing the two maps (
123
) and (

), we can define the square root virtual pullback

\sqrt{f^{!}}:A_{*}(\mathscr{Y})\to A_{*}(\mathscr{X})

It is easy to show that
\sqrt{f^{!}}
is independent of the choice of a global factorization (
124
).
Indeed, we just have to show that (
125
) is independent of (
124
), which can be seen by the arguments in
[
KiPa1
, Lem. 4.6]
.

Step 3.
Next we show that the square root virtual pullback
\sqrt{f^{!}}
in (
